KL Divergence Computation

KL divergence between two multivariate Gaussian distributions is,

\displaystyle\quad KL(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\bm{\Sigma}_{1})\ ||\ \mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\bm{\Sigma}_{2}))

\displaystyle=\int\left[\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{|\bm{\Sigma}_{2}|}{|\bm{\Sigma}_{1}|}-\frac{1}{2}(x-\mu_{1})^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}(x-\mu_{1})+\frac{1}{2}(x-\mu_{2})^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{-1}(x-\mu_{2})\right]\times p(x)dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{|\bm{\Sigma}_{2}|}{|\bm{\Sigma}_{1}|}-\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}\ \left\{E[(x-\mu_{1})(x-\mu_{1})^{\top}]\ \bm{\Sigma}_{1}^{-1}\right\}+\frac{1}{2}E[(x-\mu_{2})^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{-1}(x-\mu_{2})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{|\bm{\Sigma}_{2}|}{|\bm{\Sigma}_{1}|}-\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}\ \{\mathbf{I}_{d}\}+\frac{1}{2}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{-1}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})+\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}\{\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\log\frac{|\bm{\Sigma}_{2}|}{|\bm{\Sigma}_{1}|}-d+\text{tr}\{\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{1}\}+(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{2}^{-1}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})\right].

(47)

This formula further simplifies when
\bm{\Sigma}_{2}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{1}
share the same eigenbasis. We can write their eigendecomposition as
\bm{\Sigma}_{2}=U\Lambda_{2}U^{\top}
,
\bm{\Sigma}_{1}=U\Lambda_{1}U^{\top}
,

\displaystyle KL(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\bm{\Sigma}_{1})\ ||\ \mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\bm{\Sigma}_{2}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\log\frac{|\Lambda_{2}|}{|\Lambda_{1}|}-d+\text{tr}\{\Lambda_{2}^{-1}\Lambda_{1}\}+(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})^{\top}U\Lambda_{2}^{-1}U^{\top}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\log\frac{\prod_{k}\lambda_{2,k}}{\prod_{k}\lambda_{1,k}}-d+\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{1,k}}{\lambda_{2,k}}+(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})^{\top}U\Lambda_{2}^{-1}U^{\top}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\sum_{k}\log\frac{\lambda_{2,k}}{\lambda_{1,k}}-d+\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{1,k}}{\lambda_{2,k}}+(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})^{\top}U\Lambda_{2}^{-1}U^{\top}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})\right]

In more explicit form

\displaystyle KL(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\bm{\Sigma}_{1})\ ||\ \mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\bm{\Sigma}_{2}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\sum_{k}\log\frac{\lambda_{2,k}}{\lambda_{1,k}}-d+\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{1,k}}{\lambda_{2,k}}+(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})^{\top}\sum_{k}\frac{\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}}{\lambda_{2,k}}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}\left[\log\frac{\lambda_{2,k}}{\lambda_{1,k}}+\frac{\lambda_{1,k}}{\lambda_{2,k}}-1+\frac{\big(\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})\big)^{2}}{\lambda_{2,k}}\right]

(48)

If they share the same mean
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
it simplifies even further

\displaystyle KL=\frac{1}{2}\left[\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{1,k}}{\lambda_{2,k}}-\sum_{k}\log\frac{\lambda_{1,k}}{\lambda_{2,k}}-d\right]

(49)

which has unique minimizer when
\frac{\lambda_{1,k}}{\lambda_{2,k}}=1
.

Thus, we can compute the contribution to KL mode by mode. We denote the
contribution from mode
k
as

\displaystyle KL_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\frac{\lambda_{1,k}}{\lambda_{2,k}}-\log\frac{\lambda_{1,k}}{\lambda_{2,k}}-1)

(50)

Thus for Gaussian data that share the same mean, the KL divergence can be reduced to the ratio of generated and true variance along each principal axis of data.

Appendix D
Detailed Derivations for the One-Layer Linear Model

D.1
Zero-mean data: Exponential converging training dynamics

If
\mu=0
, then the problem reduces to
learning the covariance of data.
The gradient to weights and bias decouples

1
1

1

For notational clarity, we derive the gradient flow at a fixed noise scale
\sigma
, and omit the subscript and/or argument
\sigma
from
\mathbf{W}
,
\mathbf{b}
, and
\mathcal{L}
.

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2\mathbf{b}

(51)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-2\bm{\Sigma}+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})

(52)

The solution of
\mathbf{b}
is an exponential decay

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\mathbf{b}

\displaystyle=-2\eta\mathbf{b}

(53)

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{b}^{0}\exp(-2\eta\tau)

(54)

The solution of
\mathbf{W}
can be obtained by projecting onto eigenbasis of
\bm{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle=-2\bm{\Sigma}\mathbf{u}_{k}+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})\mathbf{u}_{k}

(55)

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}+2(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\mathbf{W}\mathbf{u}_{k}

(56)

Thus,

\frac{d}{d\tau}(\mathbf{W}\mathbf{u}_{k})=-\eta[-2\lambda_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}+2(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})(\mathbf{W}\mathbf{u}_{k})]

Define variable
\mathbf{v}_{k}=\mathbf{W}\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\mathbf{v}_{k}

\displaystyle=2\eta\lambda_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\mathbf{v}_{k}

\displaystyle=2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}-\mathbf{v}_{k}\bigg)

\mathbf{v}_{k}(\tau)=\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}+\big(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{0}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big)\exp\big(-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau\big)

Since

\displaystyle[\mathbf{v}_{k}..]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}[\mathbf{u}_{k}...]

\displaystyle VU^{\top}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

The full solution of
\mathbf{W}
reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}(\tau)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}+\sum_{k}\big(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{0}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}(1-e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau})+\sum_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{0}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}(1-e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau})+\mathbf{W}(0)\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}^{*}+\sum_{k}\big(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{0}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}^{*}+\sum_{k}\big(\mathbf{W}(0)\mathbf{u}_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}

(57)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}^{*}+\sum_{k}\big(\mathbf{W}(0)-\mathbf{W}^{*}\big)\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}

(58)

where
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{0}:=\mathbf{W}(0)\mathbf{u}_{k}
.

Remarks

•

The weight matrix
\mathbf{W}
converges to the final target mode by mode.

•

The deviation along each eigen dimension decays at different rates depending on the eigenvalue.

•

The deviation on eigen mode
\mathbf{u}_{k}
has the time constant

(2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}))^{-1}
i.e. the larger eigen dimensions
will be learned faster.

•

While the “non-resolved” dimensions will learn at the same speed

\sim(2\eta\sigma^{2})^{-1}

•

Comparing across noise scale
\sigma
, the larger noise scales will
be learned faster.

Score estimation error dynamics

Consider a target quantity of interest, i.e. difference of the score approximator from the true score.

First, under the
\mu=0
assumption, we have

\displaystyle E_{s}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\|s(\mathbf{x})-s^{*}(\mathbf{x})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{4}}\bigg[\|b-b^{*}\|^{2}+\mbox{Tr}[(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})^{\top}(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})(\bm{\Sigma}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})]\bigg]

(59)

The deviations can be expressed as

\displaystyle b-b^{*}

\displaystyle=b^{0}\exp(-2\eta\tau)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\big(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{0}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}

with the initial projection
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{0}:=\mathbf{W}(0)\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle E_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{4}}\bigg[\|b^{0}\|^{2}\exp(-4\eta\tau)+\sum_{k}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\|\mathbf{v}_{k}^{0}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\|^{2}e^{-4\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}\bigg]

(60)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{4}}\bigg[\|\delta_{b}\|^{2}\exp(-4\eta\tau)+\sum_{k}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\|\mathbf{\delta_{k}}\|^{2}e^{-4\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}\bigg]

(61)

\displaystyle\mathbf{\delta_{k}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{W}(0)\mathbf{u}_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle\delta_{b}

\displaystyle:=b^{0}

This provides us with the exact formula for error decay during training.

Denoiser estimation error dynamics

\displaystyle E_{D}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\|D(\mathbf{x})-D^{*}(\mathbf{x})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{4}E_{s}

\displaystyle=(\delta_{b})^{2}\exp(-4\eta\tau)+\sum_{k}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\|\mathbf{\delta_{k}}\|^{2}e^{-4\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}

(62)

Training loss dynamics

Under
\mu=0
assumption, the training loss is basically the true denoiser estimation error plus a constant loss floor
\sigma^{2}\mbox{Tr}[\bm{\Sigma}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})^{-1}]
determined by data covariance (trace of resolvent).

2
2

2

Note that this loss floor is a property of the data, and it’s related to the minimum MSE (MMSE) of denoising and the mutual information, we direct the interested readers to the I-MMSE relationship
[
21
]
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mu=0}=

\displaystyle\|b-(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{W})\mu\|_{2}^{2}+\mbox{Tr}[(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})^{\top}]+\sigma^{2}\mbox{Tr}[\bm{\Sigma}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})^{-1}]

\displaystyle(\mu=0)=

\displaystyle\|b\|_{2}^{2}+\mbox{Tr}[(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})^{\top}(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})]+\sigma^{2}\mbox{Tr}[\bm{\Sigma}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})^{-1}]

(63)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{D}+\sigma^{2}\mbox{Tr}[\bm{\Sigma}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})^{-1}]

(64)

D.1.1
Discrete time Gradient descent dynamics

When the dynamics is discrete-time gradient descent (GD) instead of gradient flow, we have,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2\mathbf{b}

(65)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-2\bm{\Sigma}+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})

(66)

The GD update equation reads, with
\eta
the learning rate

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{t+1}-\mathbf{b}_{t}

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{b}_{t}}\mathcal{L}

(67)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{t+1}-\mathbf{W}_{t}

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{W}_{t}}\mathcal{L}

(68)

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{t+1}

\displaystyle=(1-2\eta)\mathbf{b}_{t}

(69)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}_{t}-2\eta(-\bm{\Sigma}+\mathbf{W}_{t}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma}))

(70)

\displaystyle=2\eta\bm{\Sigma}+\mathbf{W}_{t}(\mathbf{I}-2\eta\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}-2\eta\bm{\Sigma})

(71)

\displaystyle=2\eta\bm{\Sigma}+\mathbf{W}_{t}\big((1-2\eta\sigma^{2})\mathbf{I}-2\eta\bm{\Sigma}\big)

(72)

For the weight dynamics, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{t+1}\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle=2\eta\bm{\Sigma}\mathbf{u}_{k}+\mathbf{W}_{t}\big((1-2\eta\sigma^{2})\mathbf{I}-2\eta\bm{\Sigma}\big)\mathbf{u}_{k}

(73)

\displaystyle=2\eta\lambda_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}+\mathbf{W}_{t}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big(1-2\eta\sigma^{2}-2\eta\lambda_{k}\big)

(74)

This iteration is exponentially converging to the fixed point
\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{t+1}\mathbf{u}_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle=(2\eta\lambda_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}})\mathbf{u}_{k}+\mathbf{W}_{t}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big(1-2\eta\sigma^{2}-2\eta\lambda_{k}\big)

(75)

\displaystyle=(2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})-1)\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\mathbf{u}_{k}+\mathbf{W}_{t}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big(1-2\eta\sigma^{2}-2\eta\lambda_{k}\big)

(76)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{W}_{t}\mathbf{u}_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\mathbf{u}_{k})\big(1-2\eta\sigma^{2}-2\eta\lambda_{k}\big)

(77)

Thus

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{t}\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\mathbf{u}_{k}+(\mathbf{W}_{0}\mathbf{u}_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\mathbf{u}_{k})\big(1-2\eta\sigma^{2}-2\eta\lambda_{k}\big)^{\top}

(78)

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{b}_{0}(1-2\eta)^{\top}

(79)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}+(\mathbf{W}_{0}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\mathbf{u}_{k})\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\big(1-2\eta\sigma^{2}-2\eta\lambda_{k}\big)^{\top}

(80)

So, there is no significant change from the continuous-time version.

D.1.2
Special parametrization: residual connection

Consider a special parametrization of weights

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}=c_{skip}\mathbf{I}+c_{out}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}

(81)

It’s easy to derive the dynamics of the new variables via chain rule,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathbf{W}}{\partial\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=c_{out},

(82)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=c_{out}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\mathbf{W}}.

(83)

With the original gradient

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2(\mathbf{b}-(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{W})\mu)

(84)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-2\bm{\Sigma}+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})+[2\mathbf{W}\mu\mu^{\top}+2(\mathbf{b}-\mu)\mu^{\top}]

(85)

and zero mean case

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2\mathbf{b}

(86)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-2\bm{\Sigma}+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})

(87)

the gradient to new parameters

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}=c_{out}\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2c_{out}(-\bm{\Sigma}+(c_{skip}\mathbf{I}+c_{out}\mathbf{W}^{\prime})(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma}))

(88)

\displaystyle=2c_{out}(-\bm{\Sigma}+c_{skip}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})+c_{out}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma}))

(89)

\displaystyle=2c_{out}(c_{skip}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})-\bm{\Sigma}+c_{out}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma}))

(90)

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}

(91)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\eta c_{out}}\frac{d\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=-(c_{skip}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})-\bm{\Sigma}+c_{out}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma}))

(92)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}^{\prime^{*}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{out}}(\bm{\Sigma}-c_{skip}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma}))(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})^{-1}

(93)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{out}}(\bm{\Sigma}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})^{-1}-c_{skip}\mathbf{I})

(94)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(\tau)\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(0)\mathbf{u}_{k}\exp(-2\eta\tau c_{out}^{2}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}))+

(95)

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{u}_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}-c_{skip}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}{c_{out}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}(1-\exp(-2\eta\tau c_{out}^{2}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})))

The solution to the new weights reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(\tau)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(0)-\mathbf{W}^{\prime^{*}})\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\exp(-2\eta\tau c_{out}^{2}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}))+\mathbf{W}^{\prime^{*}}

(96)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}(\tau)

\displaystyle=c_{skip}\mathbf{I}+c_{out}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(\tau)

(97)

\displaystyle=c_{skip}\mathbf{I}+c_{out}(\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(0)-\mathbf{W}^{\prime^{*}})\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\exp(-2\eta\tau c_{out}^{2}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}))+c_{out}\mathbf{W}^{\prime^{*}}

(98)

\displaystyle=\bm{\Sigma}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})^{-1}+c_{out}(\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(0)-\mathbf{W}^{\prime^{*}})\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\exp(-2\eta\tau c_{out}^{2}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}))

(99)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}^{*}+c_{out}(\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(0)-\mathbf{W}^{\prime^{*}})\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\exp(-2\eta\tau c_{out}^{2}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}))

(100)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}^{*}+(\mathbf{W}(0)-\mathbf{W}^{*})\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\exp(-2\eta\tau c_{out}^{2}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}))

(101)

The only difference is scaling the learning rate by a factor of
c_{out}^{2}
.
Also potentially depending on whether we choose to initialize
\mathbf{W}(0)
or
\mathbf{W}^{\prime}(0)
from a fixed distribution, we would get different initial values for the dynamics.

D.2
General non-centered distribution: Interaction of mean and covariance learning

Summary of results for non-centered case

When
\mu\neq 0
, the gradients to
\mathbf{W}
and
\mathbf{b}
become entangled (see Eq.
5
), resulting in a coupled linear dynamic system as follows.

Proposition D.1

(Learning dynamics of linear denoiser, non centered case)
.

Gradient flow (Eq.
GF
) is equivalent to the following ODE, with redefined dynamic variables,
\mathbf{v}_{k}(\tau)=\mathbf{W}(\tau)\mathbf{u}_{k}
,
\bar{\mathbf{b}}(\tau)=\mathbf{b}(\tau)-\mu
. Denote overlap
m_{k}:=\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\mu}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\eta}\frac{d}{d\tau}\Bigg[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{v}_{1}\\
\mathbf{v}_{2}\\
.\\
\bar{\mathbf{b}}\end{array}\Bigg]=-M\Bigg[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{v}_{1}\\
\mathbf{v}_{2}\\
.\\
\bar{\mathbf{b}}\end{array}\Bigg]+\Bigg[\begin{array}[]{c}\lambda_{1}\mathbf{u}_{1}\\
\lambda_{2}\mathbf{u}_{2}\\
.\\
0\end{array}\Bigg]

with a fixed dynamic matrix
M
defined by
\otimes
Kronecker product.

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{1}+m_{1}^{2}&m_{1}m_{2}&\hskip 9.24994pt.\hskip 9.24994pt&m_{1}\\
m_{1}m_{2}&\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{2}+m_{2}^{2}&\hskip 9.24994pt.\hskip 9.24994pt&m_{2}\\
...&...&...&.\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&.&1\end{bmatrix}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{d}

\displaystyle:=\tilde{M}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{d}

(114)

Figure 27:
Interaction of mean and covariance learning.

Top
solution to the
w,b
dynamics under different noise level

\sigma\in\{0.1,1.5,4\}
.

Bottom
Phase portraits corresponding to the two-d system.
(
m=1,\lambda_{k}=1
)

Remark D.2
.

The dynamics matrix
\tilde{M}
has a
rank-one plus diagonal structure
, specifically, it is the diagonal dynamics matrix in Eq.
7
, perturbed by the outer product of the overlap vector
m_{k}
. The eigenvalues of such matrix can be efficiently solved by numerical algebra, with eigenvectors expressed by Bunch–Nielsen–Sorensen formula
[
6
,
18
]
.
Without a closed-form formula, we have to resort to numerical simulation and low-dimensional examples to gain further insights.
We can see the coupling of
\mathbf{W}
and
\mathbf{b}
dynamics comes from the overlap of mean and principal component
m_{k}=\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\intercal}\mu
.
When certain eigenmode has no overlap,
m_{k}=0
, the corresponding weight projection
\mathbf{v}_{k}(\tau)
will follow the same dynamics as the zero-mean case, i.e. exponentially converge to the optimal solution
\lambda_{k}/(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})\mathbf{u}_{k}
.
When the overlap is non-zero
m_{k}\neq 0
, it will induce interaction between
\mathbf{v}_{k}(\tau)
and
\mathbf{b}(\tau)
and non-monotonic dynamics.

Two dimensional example

Here, we show a low-dimensional example illustrating the interaction between the bias and one eigenmode in the weight.
Consider the case where distribution mean
\mu
lies on the direction of a PC
\mathbf{u}_{k}
.
Then only the
\mathbf{u}_{k}
mode of weights interacts with the distribution mean, resulting in a two-dimensional linear system, parametrized by noise scale
\sigma
, variance of mode
\lambda
and amount of alignment
m
. Let the dynamic variable be scalars
w,b
,
\mathbf{v}_{k}=w(\tau)\mathbf{u}_{k},\mathbf{b}=b(\tau)\mathbf{u}_{k}
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\eta}\frac{d}{d\tau}\begin{bmatrix}w\\
b\end{bmatrix}=-\begin{bmatrix}m^{2}+\sigma^{2}+\lambda&m\\
m&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}w\\
b\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\lambda+m^{2}\\
m\end{bmatrix}

(115)

The phase diagram and dynamics depending on the noise scale are shown (Fig.
27
): At larger
\sigma
values, the dynamics of weights
w
will be much faster than
b
, basically,
w
gets dynamically captured by
b
, while
b
slowly relaxes to the optimal value. At small
\sigma
values, the dynamics timescale of
w
and
b
will be closer to each other, and
b
will usually have non-monotonic transient dynamics. When
\sigma^{2}
and
\lambda
are comparable,
w
will have non-monotonic dynamics.

D.2.1
Derivation of non-centered case

In this full case, the dynamics of
\mathbf{b},\mathbf{W}
are coupled

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2(\mathbf{b}-\mu+\mathbf{W}\mu)

(116)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-2\bm{\Sigma}+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})+2(\mathbf{b}-\mu+\mathbf{W}\mu)\mu^{\top}

(117)

\displaystyle=-2\bm{\Sigma}+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})+\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}\cdot\mu^{\top}

(118)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\bar{\mathcal{L}}+\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}\cdot\mu^{\top}

(119)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\bar{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle:=-2\bm{\Sigma}+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})

(120)

The nonlinear gradient learning dynamics reads

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

(121)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{W}}

\displaystyle=-\eta(\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\bar{\mathcal{L}}+\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}\cdot\mu^{\top})

(122)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{W}}-\dot{\mathbf{b}}\cdot\mu^{\top}

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\bar{\mathcal{L}}

(123)

\displaystyle=2\eta\big[\bm{\Sigma}-\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})\big]

(124)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

(125)

\displaystyle=-2\eta(\mathbf{b}-\mu+\mathbf{W}\mu)

(126)

Consider the projection

\displaystyle(\dot{\mathbf{W}}-\dot{\mathbf{b}}\cdot\mu^{\top})\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle=2\eta\big[\bm{\Sigma}-\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})\big]\mathbf{u}_{k}

(127)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{W}}\mathbf{u}_{k}-(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k})\dot{\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle=2\eta[\lambda_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\mathbf{W}\mathbf{u}_{k}]

(128)

\dot{\mathbf{b}}=-2\eta(\mathbf{b}-\mu+\sum_{k}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mu)

Consider the variables
\mathbf{v}_{k}(\tau)=\mathbf{W}(\tau)\mathbf{u}_{k}
, let
\bar{\mathbf{b}}(\tau)=\mathbf{b}(\tau)-\mu
so now the dynamic variables are
\{\mathbf{v}_{k},...,\bar{\mathbf{b}}\}

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{v}_{k}}-(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k})\dot{\bar{\mathbf{b}}}

\displaystyle=2\eta[\lambda_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\mathbf{v}_{k}]

(129)

\displaystyle\dot{\bar{\mathbf{b}}}

\displaystyle=-2\eta(\bar{\mathbf{b}}+\sum_{k}(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k})\mathbf{v}_{k})

(130)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{v}_{k}}

\displaystyle=2\eta[\lambda_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\mathbf{v}_{k}]-2\eta(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k})[\bar{\mathbf{b}}+\sum_{l}(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{l})\mathbf{v}_{l}]

(131)

\displaystyle=2\eta[\lambda_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\mathbf{v}_{k}-(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k})\bar{\mathbf{b}}-\sum_{l}(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k})(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{l})\mathbf{v}_{l}]

(132)

\displaystyle\dot{\bar{\mathbf{b}}}

\displaystyle=-2\eta(\bar{\mathbf{b}}+\sum_{k}(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k})\mathbf{v}_{k})

(133)

The whole dynamics is linear and solvable, but now the dynamics in
each component
\mathbf{v}_{k}
becomes entangled with other components
\mathbf{v}_{l}
.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\bigg[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{v}_{1}\\
\mathbf{v}_{2}\\
.\\
\bar{\mathbf{b}}\end{array}\bigg]=-2\eta M\bigg[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{v}_{1}\\
\mathbf{v}_{2}\\
.\\
\bar{\mathbf{b}}\end{array}\bigg]+2\eta\bigg[\begin{array}[]{c}\lambda_{1}\mathbf{u}_{1}\\
\lambda_{2}\mathbf{u}_{2}\\
.\\
0\end{array}\bigg]

with the blocks in
M
matrix defined as follows

\displaystyle M_{kk}

\displaystyle=(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}+(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k})^{2})\mathbf{I}

\displaystyle M_{kl}

\displaystyle=(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k})(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{l})\mathbf{I}

\displaystyle M_{kb}

\displaystyle=(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k})\mathbf{I}

\displaystyle M_{bk}

\displaystyle=(\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k})\mathbf{I}

\displaystyle M_{bb}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}

We denote the overlap between the mean and principal components as
m_{k}=\mu^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k}
,

Then we can represent the dynamic matrix
M
as a tensor product of a dense symmetric matrix
Q
with identity
\mathbf{I}_{N}
. More specifically, the dense matrix
Q
is a diagonal matrix plus the outer product of a vector. So it’s real symmetric and diagonalizable.

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{1}+m_{1}^{2}&m_{1}m_{2}&\cdots&m_{1}\\
m_{1}m_{2}&\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{2}+m_{2}^{2}&\cdots&m_{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&\cdots&1\end{array}\right]\otimes\mathbf{I}_{N}

\displaystyle=Q\otimes\mathbf{I}_{N}

(150)

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{1}+m_{1}^{2}&m_{1}m_{2}&\cdots&m_{1}\\
m_{1}m_{2}&\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{2}+m_{2}^{2}&\cdots&m_{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
m_{1}&m_{2}&\cdots&1\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=D+qq^{\top}

(155)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle:=[m_{1},m_{2},...1]^{\top}

(156)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle:=\mbox{diag}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{1},\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{2},\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{3},...\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{N},0)

(157)

Note the inverse of
Q
is analytical, but the general eigendecomposition of it is not.
Since the dynamic matrix
M
is real symmetric, the dynamics will still be separable along each eigenmode of
M
and converge w.r.t. its own eigenvalue.
The eigen decomposition of
M
can be obtained by numerical analysis
and eigenvectors from Bunch–Nielsen–Sorensen
formula
[
16
,
6
]
. Generally speaking, since the mean of dataset
\mu
usually lies in the directions of higher eigenvalues, the dynamics of
\mathbf{b}
and
\mathbf{v}_{k}
in the top eigenspace will be entangled with each other.

As a take home message, the overlap of
\mu
and spectrum of Gaussian
will induce some complex dynamics of bias and weight matrix along
these modes. The full dynamics of
\mathbf{W},\mathbf{b}
is still linear and solvable,
but since the dynamic matrix is a tensor product of identity and a diagonal-plus-low
rank matrix, a closed form solution is generally harder, we
can still obtain numerical solution of the dynamics easily.

D.2.2
Derivation of low-dimensional interaction of mean and variance learning

To gain intuition into how the mean and variance learning happens,
consider the 1d distribution case, which shares the same math as the
multi dimensional case where the mean overlaps with only one eigenmode

\displaystyle\nabla_{b}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2(b-\mu+\mathbf{W}\mu)

\displaystyle=2b-2(1-w)\mu

\displaystyle=2\mu w+2b-2\mu

(158)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-2\bm{\Sigma}+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})+2(b-\mu+\mathbf{W}\mu)\mu^{\top}

\displaystyle=-2\lambda-2(1-w)\mu^{2}+2w(\sigma^{2}+\lambda)+2\mu b

\displaystyle=2(\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2}+\lambda)w+2\mu b-2(\lambda+\mu^{2})

(159)

Write down the dynamic equation as matrix equation

\frac{d}{d\tau}\big[\begin{array}[]{c}w\\
b\end{array}\big]=-2\eta\bigg(\big[\begin{array}[]{cc}\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2}+\lambda&\ \mu\\
\mu&\ 1\end{array}\big]\ \big[\begin{array}[]{c}w\\
b\end{array}\big]-\big[\begin{array}[]{c}\lambda+\mu^{2}\\
\mu\end{array}\big]\bigg)

Eigen equation reads

\displaystyle\det(A-\gamma\mathbf{I})

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)(\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2}+\lambda-\gamma)-\mu^{2}

(160)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2}-\left(\lambda+\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2}+1\right)\gamma+\lambda+\sigma^{2}

(161)

Generally for 2x2 matrices,

\big[\begin{array}[]{cc}a&\ b\\
b&\ c\end{array}\big]

their eigenvalues are

\lambda_{1,2}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\pm\sqrt{(a-c)^{2}+4b^{2}}+a+c\right)

\mathbf{u}_{12}=\bigg[\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\pm\sqrt{(a-c)^{2}+4b^{2}}+a-c}{2b}\\
1\end{array}\bigg]

In our case,
a=\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2}+\lambda,\ b=\mu,\ c=1
.

\mathbf{u}_{12}=\bigg[\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\pm\sqrt{(\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2}+\lambda-1)^{2}+4\mu^{2}}+\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2}+\lambda-1}{2\mu}\\
1\end{array}\bigg]

The faster learning dimension will be
\lambda_{1},\mathbf{u}_{1}
,
where
w
and
b
will move in the same direction.

Key observation
is that the
w
and
b
’s dynamics depend on
\sigma
,

•

for larger
\sigma
,
w
will converge faster, while
b
will slowly
meander,
w
moving with
b
, following entrainment.

w^{*}(b)=\frac{\lambda+\mu^{2}-\mu b}{\lambda+\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2}}

•

for smaller
\sigma
,
b
will converge faster, comparable or entrained
by
w

b^{*}(w)=(1-w)\mu

D.3
Sampling ODE and Generated Distribution

For simplicity consider the zero-mean case, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}(\tau;\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}^{*}+\sum_{k}\big(\mathbf{W}(0;\sigma)\mathbf{u}_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}

(162)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}(1-e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau})+\mathbf{W}(0;\sigma)\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}

(163)

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}(\tau;\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{b}(0)\exp(-2\eta\tau)

(164)

To let it decompose mode by mode in the sampling ODE, we assume aligned
initialization
\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{W}(0;\sigma)\mathbf{u}_{m}=0
when
k\neq m
.

Then

\frac{d}{d\sigma}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}=-\frac{1}{\sigma}\bigg(\bigg(-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}+(\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{W}(0;\sigma)\mathbf{u}_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})})e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}\bigg)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}b(0;\sigma)e^{-2\eta\tau}\bigg)

Let the initialization along
\mathbf{u}_{k}
be
\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{W}(0;\sigma)\mathbf{u}_{k}=q_{k}
, then

\frac{d}{d\sigma}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}=-\frac{1}{\sigma}\bigg(\bigg(-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}+(q_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})})e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}\bigg)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}b(0;\sigma)e^{-2\eta\tau}\bigg)

Using the following integration results

\int d\sigma\frac{1}{\sigma(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}\sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\right)+C

\int d\sigma\frac{1}{\sigma}e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\sigma^{2}\right)+C

\int d\sigma\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})}e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\tau}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\sigma^{2}\right)e^{-2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}-\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\left(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\right)\right)\right)+C

Integrating this ODE, we get

\displaystyle c_{k}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=C\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\sigma^{2}\right)e^{-2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}-\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\left(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\right)\right)\right)+

(165)

\displaystyle\quad-q_{k}\frac{1}{2}\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\sigma^{2}\right)e^{-2\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}\bigg)

(166)

\displaystyle=C\sqrt{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\left((1-q_{k})\ \text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\sigma^{2}\right)e^{-2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}-\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\left(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\right)\right)\right)\bigg)

(167)

Solution of sampling dynamics ODE

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}(\sigma_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}\frac{c_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{c_{k}(\sigma_{T})}(\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}(\sigma_{T}))

(168)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{c_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{c_{k}(\sigma_{T})}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}(\sigma_{T})

(169)

The variance of generated distribution reads

\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}^{\tau}=\sigma_{T}^{2}\sum_{k}(\frac{c_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{c_{k}(\sigma_{T})})^{2}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

(170)

where the variance along eigenvector
\mathbf{u}_{k}
reads

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{k}^{\tau}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{T}^{2}(\frac{c_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{c_{k}(\sigma_{T})})^{2}

(171)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{T}^{2}\frac{\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{0}^{2}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{T}^{2}}\frac{\exp\bigg((1-q_{k})\ \text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\sigma_{0}^{2}\right)e^{-2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}-\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\left(\sigma_{0}^{2}+\lambda_{k}\right)\right)\bigg)}{\exp\bigg((1-q_{k})\ \text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\sigma_{T}^{2}\right)e^{-2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}-\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\left(\sigma_{T}^{2}+\lambda_{k}\right)\right)\bigg)}

(172)

Appendix E
Detailed Derivations for Two-Layer Symmetric Parameterization

Here we outline the main derivation steps for the two-layer symmetric
case:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{x})\;=\;P\,P^{\top}\,\mathbf{x}\;+\;\mathbf{b}.

(173)

E.1
Symmetric parametrization zero mean gradient dynamics

Note that if the weight matrix
\mathbf{W}
has internal structure, i.e., parametrized by
\theta
, we can easily derive the gradient flow of those parameters using the chain rule.

Let
\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{W}(\theta)
,

\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}=\sum_{ij}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\mathbf{W}}\right)_{ij}\frac{\partial\mathbf{W}_{ij}}{\partial\theta}

(174)

Here, when
\mathbf{W}=PP^{\top}
, via the chain rule, we can derive its gradient, which depends on the symmetrized gradient of
\mathbf{W}

\displaystyle\nabla_{P}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L})P+(\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L})^{\top}P

(175)

\displaystyle=\bigg[\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}+(\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L})^{\top}\bigg]P

(176)

where

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2(\mathbf{b}-(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{W})\mu)

(177)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-2\bm{\Sigma}+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})+[2\mathbf{W}\mu\mu^{\top}+2(\mathbf{b}-\mu)\mu^{\top}]

(178)

Expanding the full gradient (non-zero mean case), we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{P}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\bigg[-4\bm{\Sigma}+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})+[2\mathbf{W}\mu\mu^{\top}+2(b-\mu)\mu^{\top}]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})\mathbf{W}^{\top}+[2\mu\mu^{\top}\mathbf{W}^{\top}+2\mu(b-\mu)^{\top}]\bigg]P

(179)

\displaystyle=-4\bm{\Sigma}P+2PP^{\top}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})P+2(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})PP^{\top}P

\displaystyle\quad\quad+[2PP^{\top}\mu\mu^{\top}+2(b-\mu)\mu^{\top}]P+[2\mu\mu^{\top}PP^{\top}+2\mu(b-\mu)^{\top}]P

(180)

In the zero-mean case this simplifies to

\displaystyle\nabla_{P}\mathcal{L}_{\mu=0}

\displaystyle=-4\bm{\Sigma}P+2PP^{\top}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})P+2(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})PP^{\top}P

(181)

Consider representing the gradient on eigenbases, let
\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}P=q_{k}^{\top}
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\nabla_{P}\mathcal{L}_{\mu=0}

\displaystyle=-4\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}P+2\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}PP^{\top}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})P+2\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\bm{\Sigma})PP^{\top}P

(182)

\displaystyle=-4\lambda_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}P+2\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}P\sum_{m}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m})P^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{m}\mathbf{u}_{m}^{\top}P+2(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}PP^{\top}P

(183)

\displaystyle=-4\lambda_{k}q_{k}^{\top}+2q_{k}^{\top}\sum_{m}(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m})q_{m}q_{m}^{\top}+2(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})q_{k}^{\top}\sum_{m}q_{m}q_{m}^{\top}

(184)

\displaystyle=-4\lambda_{k}q_{k}^{\top}+2\sum_{m}\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})q_{m}^{\top}

(185)

\displaystyle\nabla_{q_{k}^{\top}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu=0}

\displaystyle=-4\lambda_{k}q_{k}^{\top}+2\sum_{m}\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})q_{m}^{\top}

(186)

\displaystyle\nabla_{q_{k}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu=0}

\displaystyle=-4\lambda_{k}q_{k}+2\sum_{m}\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})q_{m}

(187)

Fixed points analysis

A stationary solution at which the gradient vanishes is

q_{k}^{\top}q_{m}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }k\neq m,\\[4.0pt]
\dfrac{\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\text{ or }0,&\text{if }k=m.\end{cases}

(188)

Note, this is different from the one-layer case where there are no
saddle points; here we get a bunch of zero solutions as saddle points.

Dynamics of the overlap

Note the dynamics of the overlap

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})

\displaystyle=q_{k}^{\top}\frac{d}{d\tau}q_{m}+q_{m}^{\top}\frac{d}{d\tau}q_{k}

\displaystyle=-\eta[q_{k}^{\top}\nabla_{q_{m}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu=0}+q_{m}^{\top}\nabla_{q_{k}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu=0}]

\displaystyle=-\eta[-4\lambda_{k}(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})+2\sum_{n}\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{n}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{n})q_{m}^{\top}q_{n}

\displaystyle\quad-4\lambda_{m}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})+2\sum_{n}\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{n}+\lambda_{m}\big)(q_{m}^{\top}q_{n})q_{k}^{\top}q_{n}]

\displaystyle=-\eta[-4(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})+2\sum_{n}\big(4\sigma^{2}+2\lambda_{n}+\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{n})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{n})]

\displaystyle=4\eta[(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})-\sum_{n}\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{n}+\frac{\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{n})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{n})]

(189)

This shows that when all the overlaps are initialized as zero, they will stay at zero, i.e., when weights are initialized to be aligned to the eigenbasis, they will stay aligned.
We will first solve the aligned case analytically in Sec.
E.1.1
, and then analyze the dynamics of overlap qualitatively in Sec.
E.1.2
.

E.1.1
Simplifying assumption: orthogonal initialization
q_{k}^{\top}q_{m}=0

Consider the simple case where each
q_{k}^{\top}q_{m}=0,\ \forall k\neq m

at network initialization, i.e., each
q
are orthogonal to each other.
Then, it’s easy to show that
\frac{d}{d\tau}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})=0
at the start and throughout training. Thus, we know orthogonally initialized modes will evolve independently.

Note this assumption can also be written as

q_{k}^{\top}q_{m}=\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}PP^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{m}=\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{u}_{m}=0\quad\forall k\neq m

(190)

which means the eigenvectors of
\bm{\Sigma}
are still orthogonal w.r.t. matrix
\mathbf{W}
, i.e., the matrix
\mathbf{W}
shares eigenbases with the data covariance
\bm{\Sigma}
.

In such case, the gradient reads

\displaystyle\nabla_{q_{k}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu=0}=

\displaystyle-4\lambda_{k}q_{k}+2\sum_{m}\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})q_{m}

(191)

\displaystyle(\text{ortho \ref{eq:two_layer_ortho_assumption}})=

\displaystyle-4\lambda_{k}q_{k}+2\big(2\sigma^{2}+2\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})q_{k}

(192)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\lambda_{k}q_{k}+4\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})q_{k}

(193)

The dynamics read

\displaystyle\frac{dq_{k}}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla_{q_{k}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu=0}

(194)

\displaystyle=-\eta\left(-4\lambda_{k}q_{k}+4\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})q_{k}\right)

(195)

\displaystyle=4\eta\left(\lambda_{k}-\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})\right)q_{k}

(196)

Since the right hand side is aligned with
q_{k}
, it can only
move by scaling the initial value.

The fixed points of the dynamics are given by
q_{k}=0
or when
q_{k}^{\top}q_{k}=\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}.

Given the arbitrariness of
q_{k}
itself, we track the dynamics of
its squared norm. The learning dynamics of
q_{k}^{\top}q_{k}
read

\displaystyle q_{k}^{\top}\frac{dq_{k}}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=4\eta(\lambda_{k}-\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k}))(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})

(197)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=4\eta(\lambda_{k}-\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k}))(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})

(198)

\displaystyle\frac{d(f)}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=8\eta(\lambda_{k}-\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big)f)f

(199)

Fortunately, this ODE is a specific case of the Riccati equation, which admits a closed-form solution.

\displaystyle(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})(\tau)=\|q_{k}(\tau)\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{1/Ke^{-at}+b}

(200)

\displaystyle=\frac{8\eta\lambda_{k}}{1/Ke^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+8\eta\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big)}

(201)

\displaystyle=\frac{8\eta\lambda_{k}}{(\frac{8\eta\lambda_{k}}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}-8\eta\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big))e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+8\eta\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big)}

(202)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}-\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big))e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big)}

(203)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\bigg(\frac{1}{(\frac{1}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+1}\bigg)

(204)

\displaystyle(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})(\tau)

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{if }k\neq m

(205)

This gives rise to the full vector solution

\displaystyle q_{k}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\|q_{k}(\tau)\|^{2}}\frac{q_{k}(0)}{\|q_{k}(0)\|}

(206)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\frac{1}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+1}}\bigg)\frac{q_{k}(0)}{\|q_{k}(0)\|}

(207)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2})e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}}\bigg)q_{k}(0)

(208)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+(1-e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau})\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}}\bigg)q_{k}(0)

(209)

Learning Dynamics of score estimator

Now, reconstruct the whole estimator,
\mathbf{W}=PP^{\top}
. Recall
\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}P=q_{k}^{\top}

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}P

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}q_{k}^{\top}

\displaystyle PP^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}q_{k}^{\top})(\sum_{m}q_{m}\mathbf{u}_{m}^{\top})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sum_{m}\mathbf{u}_{k}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})\mathbf{u}_{m}^{\top}

Note that under our assumption,
(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})(\tau)=0\quad\text{if }k\neq m
,

q_{k}^{\top}q_{m}
is diagonal. So

\displaystyle PP^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\|q_{k}(\tau)\|^{2}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

Then we can rewrite

\displaystyle\|q_{k}(\tau)\|^{2}=(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})(\tau)=\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\bigg(\frac{1}{(\frac{1}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+1}\bigg)

(210)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}(\tau)=PP^{\top}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\|q_{k}(\tau)\|^{2}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\bigg(\frac{1}{(\frac{1}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+1}\bigg)

(211)

Score estimation error dynamics

\displaystyle E_{s}=\mathbf{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\|s(\mathbf{x})-s^{*}(\mathbf{x})\|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{4}}\bigg[\|\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{b}^{*}\|^{2}+2(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{b}^{*})^{\top}(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})\mathbf{\mu}

(212)

\displaystyle\quad+\mbox{Tr}[(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})^{\top}(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})(\mu\mu^{\top}+\bm{\Sigma}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})]\bigg]

\displaystyle E_{s}^{(\mu=0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{4}}\bigg[\|\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{b}^{*}\|^{2}+\mbox{Tr}[(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})^{\top}(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})(\bm{\Sigma}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})]\bigg]

(213)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{4}}\bigg[\|\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{b}^{*}\|^{2}+\mbox{Tr}[(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})^{\top}(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}]\bigg]

(214)

Thus

\displaystyle E_{s}^{(\mu=0,\text{ cov term})}

\displaystyle=\mbox{Tr}[(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})^{\top}(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}]

(215)

\displaystyle=\mbox{Tr}[\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}(\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{W}^{*})^{\top}]

(216)

\displaystyle=\mbox{Tr}\bigg[\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})\big(Pq_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big)\big(Pq_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big)^{\top}\bigg]

(217)

\displaystyle=\mbox{Tr}\bigg[\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})\big(Pq_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big)^{\top}\big(Pq_{k}-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big)\bigg]

(218)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})\big(q_{k}^{\top}P^{\top}Pq_{k}-2\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}Pq_{k}+(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}})^{2}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{k}\big)

(219)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})\big((q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})^{2}-2\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}q_{k}^{\top}q_{k}+(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}})^{2}\big)

(220)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})\big((q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\big)^{2}

(221)

E_{s}^{(\mu=0,\text{ bias term})}=(b^{0})^{2}\exp(-4\eta\tau)

The dynamics can be applied

(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})(\tau)=\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\bigg(\frac{1}{(\frac{1}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+1}\bigg)

\displaystyle E_{s}^{(\mu=0,\text{ cov term})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})\big(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\bigg(\frac{1}{(\frac{1}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+1}\bigg)-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\big)^{2}

(222)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2})(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}})^{2}\big(\bigg(\frac{1}{(\frac{1}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+1}\bigg)-1\big)^{2}

(223)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}^{2}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\bigg(\frac{(\frac{1}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}}{(\frac{1}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+1}\bigg)^{2}

(224)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}^{2}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\bigg(\frac{(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2})e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}}{(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2})e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\bigg)^{2}

(225)

The full score estimation loss is

\displaystyle E_{s}^{(\mu=0)}

\displaystyle=(b^{0})^{2}\exp(-4\eta\tau)+

(226)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}^{2}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}}\bigg(\frac{(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2})e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}}{(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2})e^{-8\eta\lambda_{k}\tau}+\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\bigg)^{2}

E.1.2
Beyond Aligned Initialization: qualitative analysis of off diagonal
dynamics

Next we can write down the dynamics of the non-diagonal part of the weight, i.e., overlaps between
q_{k}
and
q_{m}

\displaystyle\quad\frac{d}{d\tau}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})

(227)

\displaystyle=q_{k}^{\top}\frac{d}{d\tau}q_{m}+q_{m}^{\top}\frac{d}{d\tau}q_{k}

(228)

\displaystyle=-\eta[-4(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})+2\sum_{n}\big(4\sigma^{2}+2\lambda_{n}+\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{n})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{n})]

(229)

\displaystyle=4\eta[(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})-\sum_{n}\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{n}+\frac{\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{n})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{n})]

(230)

\displaystyle=4\eta\bigg[(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})-\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}+\frac{\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

(231)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m}+\frac{\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{m})

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\sum_{n\neq k,m}\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{n}+\frac{\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{n})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{n})\bigg]

\displaystyle=4\eta\bigg[\bigg(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m}-\big(2\sigma^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{m}+3\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)\|q_{k}\|^{2}-\big(2\sigma^{2}+\frac{3\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)\|q_{m}\|^{2}\bigg)(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

(232)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\sum_{n\neq k,m}\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{n}+\frac{\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{n})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{n})\bigg]

\displaystyle=4\eta\bigg[\bigg(\lambda_{k}(1-\|q_{k}\|^{2})+\lambda_{m}(1-\|q_{m}\|^{2})-\big(2\sigma^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)(\|q_{k}\|^{2}+\|q_{m}\|^{2})\bigg)(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

(233)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\sum_{n\neq k,m}\big(2\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{n}+\frac{\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{n})(q_{m}^{\top}q_{n})\bigg]

As a reference, recall the dynamics of diagonal term,

\frac{d(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})}{d\tau}=8\eta\Big(\lambda_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})\Big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})

(234)

Assume the diagonal term is not stuck at zero
q_{k}^{\top}q_{k}\neq 0
, then we know it will asymptotically go to
\|q_{k}(\infty)\|^{2}\approx\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}
following sigmoidal dynamics.

For overlap between
q_{k}^{\top}q_{m}
, without loss of generality, assume
\lambda_{k}>\lambda_{m}
. Then we have three dynamic phases: 1) neither mode has emerged; 2) mode
k
has emerged, while
m
has not; 3) both modes have emerged.

Phase 1: neither mode has emerged

When neither mode has emerged, assume
\|q_{k}(\tau)\|^{2}\approx 0
, then the overlap will grow exponentially.

\frac{d}{d\tau}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})\approx 4\eta\bigg(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m}\bigg)(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

Note given
\lambda_{k}>\lambda_{m}
, the diagonal term has a higher increasing speed than the non-diagonal overlap term
8\eta(\lambda_{k}-\big(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}\big)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k}))>4\eta\big(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m}\big)
.

Phase 2: one mode has emerged, the other has not

When one mode has converged
\|q_{k}(\tau)\|^{2}\approx\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}
, but the other has not (
\lambda_{k}>\lambda_{m}
)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})

\displaystyle\approx 4\eta\bigg(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m}-\big(2\sigma^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{m}+3\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\bigg)(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

(235)

\displaystyle=4\eta\bigg(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m}-\big(2\sigma^{2}+2\lambda_{k}+\frac{\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\bigg)(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

(236)

\displaystyle=4\eta\bigg(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m}-2\lambda_{k}-\big(\frac{\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\bigg)(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

(237)

\displaystyle=4\eta\bigg(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{k}-\big(\frac{\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\bigg)(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

(238)

\displaystyle=4\eta(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{k})\bigg(1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\bigg)(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

(239)

Since
\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}<1
, we know
1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}>0
. Thus, the dynamic coefficient
(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{k})\big(1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\big)<0
, so the overlap will follow an exponential decay dynamics.
Further, given the same
\lambda_{k}
, the decay speed of
q_{k}^{\top}q_{m}
is proportional to the difference of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{k}
. So, the larger the difference, the faster the decay.

Phase 3: both modes have emerged

When both modes have emerged
\|q_{k}(\tau)\|^{2}\approx\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}
,
\|q_{m}(\tau)\|^{2}\approx\frac{\lambda_{m}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})

\displaystyle\approx 4\eta\bigg(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m}-\big(2\sigma^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{m}+3\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-\big(2\sigma^{2}+\frac{3\lambda_{m}+\lambda_{k}}{2}\big)\frac{\lambda_{m}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m}}\bigg)(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

(240)

\displaystyle=4\eta\bigg(-\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{m}+\big(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\big)(\frac{(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{k})^{2}}{(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m})(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})})\bigg)(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

(241)

\displaystyle=-4\eta\frac{\left(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m}+2\sigma^{2}\right)\left(2\lambda_{m}\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\left(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{m}\right)\right)}{2\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\right)\left(\lambda_{m}+\sigma^{2}\right)}(q_{m}^{\top}q_{k})

(242)

The dynamic coefficient is negative, so the overlap decays even more rapidly.

Summary: Qualitative description of off diagonal elements
q_{k}^{\top}q_{m}
dynamics

•

The off-diagonal term will exponentially rise after the rise of the larger variance dimension, and before the rise of smaller variance dimension;

•

after one has risen it will decay to zero;

•

after both have risen it will decay faster.

Thus, it will follow non-monotonic rise and fall dynamics.

E.2
Sampling ODE and Generated Distribution

Here, for tractability purposes, we will focus on the zero-mean and aligned initialization case.

Recall the weight learning solutions above (
211
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}(\tau;\sigma)=PP^{\top}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\|q_{k}(\tau)\|^{2}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\bigg((\frac{1}{\|q_{k}(0;\sigma)\|^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+1\bigg)^{-1}

(243)

with the weight initialization

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}(0;\sigma)=\sum_{k}\|q_{k}(0;\sigma)\|^{2}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

(244)

Note here we assume the initialization
\|q_{k}(0;\sigma)\|^{2}

is the same for all
\sigma
levels, with no
\sigma
dependency.

\|q_{k}(0;\sigma)\|^{2}=\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}=Q_{k}

Per our general analysis (Sec.
C.5
), the key factors are

\displaystyle\psi_{k}(\sigma;\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\frac{1}{(\frac{1}{Q_{k}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+1}

(245)

and its integration

\Phi_{k}(\sigma)=\exp\bigl(-\int^{\sigma}\frac{\psi_{k}(\sigma^{\prime};\tau)-1}{\sigma^{\prime}}d\sigma^{\prime}\bigr)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\sigma}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sigma}\bigg(\psi_{k}(\sigma;\tau)-1\bigg)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}

(246)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sigma}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\frac{1}{(\frac{1}{\|q_{k}(0)\|^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+1}-1\bigg)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}

(247)

Note that the integrand is just a fractional function of
\sigma^{2}

which can be integrated analytically.

\displaystyle\int d\sigma-\frac{1}{\sigma}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}\frac{1}{(\frac{1}{Q_{k}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}-1)e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+1}-1\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{Q_{k}e^{8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\log\left(\lambda_{k}+Q_{k}\left(e^{8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}-1\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\right)\right)-2Q_{k}\log(\sigma)+2\log(\sigma)}{2\left(e^{8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}-1\right)Q_{k}+2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{Q_{k}e^{8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\log\left(\lambda_{k}+Q_{k}\left(e^{8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}-1\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\right)\right)+2(1-Q_{k})\log(\sigma)}{2\left(e^{8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}-1\right)Q_{k}+2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{Q_{k}\log\left(\lambda_{k}+Q_{k}\left(e^{8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}-1\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\right)\right)+2(1-Q_{k})\log(\sigma)e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}{2\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)Q_{k}+2e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{Q_{k}\log[e^{8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\left(\lambda_{k}e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+Q_{k}\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\right)\right)]+2(1-Q_{k})\log(\sigma)e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}{2Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{Q_{k}\log\left(\lambda_{k}e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+Q_{k}\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\right)\right)+8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})\log(\sigma)e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}{2Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}

Thus, the general solution to the sampling ODE is

\displaystyle c_{k}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=C\ \exp(\frac{Q_{k}\log\left(\lambda_{k}e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+Q_{k}\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\right)\right)+8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})\log(\sigma)e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}{2Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}})

\displaystyle=C\ \bigg[\lambda_{k}e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+Q_{k}\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\right)\bigg]^{\frac{Q_{k}}{2Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}}\times

\displaystyle\quad\exp\big(\frac{8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}Q_{k}}{2Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}\big)\exp\big(\frac{(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\log(\sigma)}{Q_{k}+(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}\big)

(248)

The scaling ratio is

\displaystyle\frac{c_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{c_{k}(\sigma_{T})}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\frac{\lambda_{k}e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+Q_{k}\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{0}^{2}\right)}{\lambda_{k}e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+Q_{k}\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{T}^{2}\right)}\bigg]^{\frac{Q_{k}}{2Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}}

(249)

\displaystyle\quad\exp\big(\frac{(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}(\log(\sigma_{0})-\log(\sigma_{T}))}{Q_{k}+(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg[\frac{\lambda_{k}e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+Q_{k}\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{0}^{2}\right)}{\lambda_{k}e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+Q_{k}\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{T}^{2}\right)}\bigg]^{\frac{Q_{k}}{2Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}}\ \big(\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma_{T}}\big)^{\frac{(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}{Q_{k}+(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}}

(250)

\displaystyle\Phi_{k}(\sigma)=\bigg[\lambda_{k}e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+Q_{k}\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma^{2}\right)\bigg]^{\frac{Q_{k}}{2Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}}\big(\sigma\big)^{\frac{(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}{Q_{k}+(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}}

(251)

Evolution of distribution

Following the derivation above,

\bm{\Sigma}[\mathbf{x}(\sigma_{0})]=\sum_{k}\left(\sigma_{T}\frac{c_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{c_{k}(\sigma_{T})}\right)^{2}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

The generated variance along eigenvector
\mathbf{u}_{k}
is

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{k}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\left(\sigma_{T}\frac{c_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{c_{k}(\sigma_{T})}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{T}^{2}\bigg[\frac{\lambda_{k}e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+Q_{k}\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{0}^{2}\right)}{\lambda_{k}e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}+Q_{k}\left(1-e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{T}^{2}\right)}\bigg]^{\frac{Q_{k}}{Q_{k}+(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}}\big(\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma_{T}}\big)^{\frac{2(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}{Q_{k}+(1-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}}}

(252)

Asymptotics of Learning: early and late training

At late training stage
\eta\tau\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\tau\to\infty}\frac{c_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{c_{k}(\sigma_{T})}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\frac{Q_{k}\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{0}^{2}\right)}{Q_{k}\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{T}^{2}\right)}\bigg]^{\frac{Q_{k}}{2Q_{k}}}\exp\big(\frac{0}{Q_{k}}\big)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{0}^{2}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{T}^{2}}}

(253)

\lim_{\eta\tau\to\infty}\tilde{\lambda}_{k}(\tau)=\sigma_{T}^{2}\frac{\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{0}^{2}}{\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{T}^{2}}

(254)

which approximately recovers the correct scaling factor to generate correct variance
\lambda_{k}
, if we take
\sigma_{T}\to\infty,\sigma_{0}\to 0
.

At early training stage
\eta\tau\to 0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\tau\to 0}\frac{c_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{c_{k}(\sigma_{T})}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\frac{\lambda_{k}1+Q_{k}\left(1-1\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{0}^{2}\right)}{\lambda_{k}1+Q_{k}\left(1-1\right)\left(\lambda_{k}+\sigma_{T}^{2}\right)}\bigg]^{\frac{Q_{k}}{2Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})}}\big(\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma_{T}}\big)^{\frac{(1-Q_{k})}{Q_{k}+(1-Q_{k})}}

\displaystyle=\bigg[1\bigg]^{\frac{Q_{k}}{2Q_{k}+2(1-Q_{k})}}(\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma_{T}})^{1-Q_{k}}

\displaystyle=(\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma_{T}})^{1-Q_{k}}

(255)

which shows the initial generated variance is determined by weight initialization scale and integration limits
(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{T})
.

Appendix F
Detailed Derivations for Deep Linear Network

Consider a general deep linear network.
\mathbf{W}=\prod_{i}\mathbf{W}_{i}=\mathbf{W}_{L}\mathbf{W}_{L-1}...\mathbf{W}_{1}

Gradient structure

Using the chain rule

\nabla_{y}\mathcal{L}=\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\mathbf{W}}\right)_{ij}\frac{\partial\mathbf{W}_{ij}}{\partial y}

we have the gradient with respect to matrix
\mathbf{W}_{l}

\frac{\partial\mathbf{W}_{mn}}{\partial\mathbf{W}_{l,ij}}=(\mathbf{W}_{L}...\mathbf{W}_{l+1})_{mi}(\mathbf{W}_{l-1}...\mathbf{W}_{1})_{jn}

In vector notation,

\displaystyle\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{W}_{l}}\mathcal{L}\right]_{ij}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\mathbf{W}}\right)_{mn}\frac{\partial\mathbf{W}_{mn}}{\partial\mathbf{W}_{l,ij}}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{W}_{L}\ldots\mathbf{W}_{l+1})_{mi}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\mathbf{W}}\right)_{mn}(\mathbf{W}_{l-1}\ldots\mathbf{W}_{1})_{jn}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}_{l}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{W}_{L}\ldots\mathbf{W}_{l+1})^{\top}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\mathbf{W}}\right)(\mathbf{W}_{l-1}\ldots\mathbf{W}_{1})^{\top}

where
\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}
can be found in (
5
)

\displaystyle\nabla_{b}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2(b-(I-\mathbf{W})\mu)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-2\Sigma+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)+[2\mathbf{W}\mu\mu^{\top}+2(b-\mu)\mu^{\top}]

F.1
Aligned assumption

This gradient structure can be substantially simplified if the weight matrices are aligned at initialization.

Consider the singular value decomposition of each weight matrix
\mathbf{W}_{l}

\mathbf{W}_{l}=U_{l}\Lambda_{l}V_{l}^{\top}

(256)

and for each pair of neighboring layers, the singular modes are aligned.

V_{l}^{\top}U_{l-1}=I

(257)

or equivalently
U_{l-1}=V_{l},\ \forall l\in[2,L]

Gradient structure under aligned assumption

Then the product of weight matrices is

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{L}\ldots\mathbf{W}_{l+1}

\displaystyle=U_{L}\left(\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}\right)V_{l+1}^{\top}

(258)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{l-1}\ldots\mathbf{W}_{1}

\displaystyle=U_{l-1}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}\right)V_{1}^{\top}

(259)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{L}\ldots\mathbf{W}_{1}

\displaystyle=U_{L}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}\right)V_{1}^{\top}

(260)

Then the evolution of hidden weights reads

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}_{l}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=(U_{L}\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}V_{l+1}^{\top})^{\top}\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}(U_{l-1}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}V_{1}^{\top})^{\top}

\displaystyle=V_{l+1}\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}U_{L}^{\top}\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}V_{1}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}U_{l-1}^{\top}

\displaystyle=U_{l}\bigg[\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}U_{L}^{\top}\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}V_{1}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}\bigg]V_{l}^{\top}

(261)

Substituting the gradient of the loss (
5
), assuming centered data
\mu=0
,

\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu=0}=-2\Sigma+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)

(262)

Then

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}_{l}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=U_{l}\bigg[\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}U_{L}^{\top}\big(-2\Sigma+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)\big)V_{1}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}\bigg]V_{l}^{\top}

\displaystyle=U_{l}\bigg[\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}U_{L}^{\top}\big(-2\Sigma+2U_{L}\prod_{k=1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}V_{1}^{\top}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)\big)V_{1}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}\bigg]V_{l}^{\top}

\displaystyle=-2U_{l}\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}U_{L}^{\top}\Sigma V_{1}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}V_{l}^{\top}+2\sigma^{2}U_{l}\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}\prod_{k=1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}V_{l}^{\top}

\displaystyle+2U_{l}\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}\prod_{k=1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}V_{1}^{\top}\Sigma V_{1}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}V_{l}^{\top}

Under the simplifying assumption that
U_{L}=V_{1}
and they exactly match the eigenbasis of
\Sigma
, denoted as
U
, then

V_{1}^{\top}\Sigma V_{1}=\Lambda,\quad U_{L}^{\top}\Sigma V_{1}=\Lambda

(263)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}_{l}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-2U_{l}\Lambda\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}V_{l}^{\top}+2\sigma^{2}U_{l}\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}\prod_{k=1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}V_{l}^{\top}+2U_{l}\prod_{k=l+1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}\prod_{k=1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}\Lambda\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\Lambda_{k}V_{l}^{\top}

(264)

\displaystyle=2U_{l}[-\Lambda+\sigma^{2}\prod_{k=1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}+\Lambda\prod_{k=1}^{L}\Lambda_{k}]\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k}V_{l}^{\top}

(265)

\displaystyle=2U_{l}[-\Lambda+(\sigma^{2}+\Lambda)\Lambda_{l}\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k}]\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k}V_{l}^{\top}

(266)

Consider the singular values of each weight matrix

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Lambda_{l}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=U_{l}^{\top}\nabla_{\mathbf{W}_{l}}\mathcal{L}V_{l}

\displaystyle=2[-\Lambda+(\sigma^{2}+\Lambda)\Lambda_{l}\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k}]\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k}

The weight dynamics have a surprisingly simple mode-by-mode form.
For the
m
-th mode, the gradient is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Lambda_{l,m}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2[-\lambda_{m}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m})\Lambda_{l,m}\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k,m}]\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k,m}

To simplify notation, we define
\Xi_{l,m}:=\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k,m}
. Then the gradient flow dynamics reads

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\Lambda_{l,m}

\displaystyle=-\eta[-\lambda_{m}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m})\Lambda_{l,m}\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k,m}]\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k,m}

\displaystyle=-\eta[-\lambda_{m}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m})\Lambda_{l,m}\Xi_{l,m}]\Xi_{l,m}

At first glance, this is a linear system for
\Lambda_{l,m}
, with a fixed point at
\lambda_{m}/\big[(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{m})\,\Xi_{l,m}\big]
.
But the effect of other layers
\Xi_{l,m}
is also dynamic, forming a coupled nonlinear system.

\Xi_{l,m}
affects both the time constant and convergence level.

F.2
Two layer linear network

We start by considering the two-layer case of this equation.
Under the aligned initialization assumption,

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{1}

\displaystyle=U_{1}\Lambda_{1}V_{1}^{\top},\quad

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{2}

\displaystyle=U_{2}\Lambda_{2}V_{2}^{\top}

(267)

\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle=V_{2},\quad

\displaystyle U_{2}

\displaystyle=V_{1}=U

(268)

So the main degrees of freedom come from
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
, and we discuss the two cases depending on whether they are equal to each other.

F.2.1
Special case: homogeneous initialization
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{2}
(symmetric two layer case)

If
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{2}
, then

\mathbf{W}_{1}=U_{1}\Lambda_{1}V_{1}^{\top}=V_{2}\Lambda_{2}U_{2}^{\top}=\mathbf{W}_{2}^{\top}

We are reduced to the symmetric two-layer case
\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{W}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{1}
as we discussed above in
E
.
Due to weight sharing, the gradients to each layer are accumulated.

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Lambda_{1}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2[-\Lambda+(\sigma^{2}+\Lambda)\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2}]\Lambda_{2}

\displaystyle=2[-\Lambda+(\sigma^{2}+\Lambda)\Lambda_{1}^{2}]\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle=-2\eta[-\Lambda+(\sigma^{2}+\Lambda)\Lambda_{1}^{2}]\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}(\Lambda_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle=-4\eta[-\Lambda+(\sigma^{2}+\Lambda)\Lambda_{1}^{2}]\Lambda_{1}^{2}

This recovers the dynamics we obtained in the previous section
5.1
.

F.2.2
General Case: general initialization (general two layer
\mathbf{W}=PQ
)

Generally, if the initialization is not homogeneous,
\Lambda_{1}\neq\Lambda_{2}
, the matrices are not the same
\mathbf{W}=PQ
, and we have the general two-layer parametrization.

Under the aligned assumption
F.1
, the weight learning dynamics are reduced to those of
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
. For simplicity of notation, let
f_{k}=\Lambda_{1,k},g_{k}=\Lambda_{2,k}
. We have

\displaystyle\nabla_{f_{k}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{k}g_{k}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})g_{k}^{2}f_{k}

(269)

\displaystyle\nabla_{g_{k}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{k}f_{k}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})f_{k}^{2}g_{k}

(270)

which is a two-dimensional coupled system.
Let us focus on the dynamics along one eigenmode
k
and drop the subscript
k
from
f_{k},g_{k}
.
Let the constants
A=\eta\lambda_{k},B=\eta(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}f

\displaystyle=Ag-Bg^{2}f

(271)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}g

\displaystyle=Af-Bf^{2}g

(272)

There are three important variables:
f_{k}g_{k}
,
f_{k}^{2}+g_{k}^{2}
, and
f^{2}-g^{2}
.
This system can be represented in the following way, which exposes its conserved quantity:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}(fg)

\displaystyle=f\frac{d}{d\tau}g+g\frac{d}{d\tau}f

\displaystyle=Af^{2}-Bf^{3}g+Ag^{2}-Bg^{3}f

\displaystyle=(f^{2}+g^{2})\ (A-Bfg)

(273)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}(f^{2}+g^{2})

\displaystyle=2f\frac{d}{d\tau}f+2g\frac{d}{d\tau}g

\displaystyle=2(Afg-Bg^{2}f^{2})+2(Afg-Bf^{2}g^{2})

\displaystyle=2\ fg\ (A-Bfg)

(274)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}(f^{2}-g^{2})

\displaystyle=0

(275)

Thus the
(f,g)
pair can only flow along hyperbolic lines or diagonal lines where
f^{2}-g^{2}=\text{const}
.
We can leverage this fact to write down the overall solution.

Notice that

(f^{2}+g^{2})^{2}-(f^{2}-g^{2})^{2}=4f^{2}g^{2}

(276)

So we can represent

\displaystyle f^{2}+g^{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{4f^{2}g^{2}+(f^{2}-g^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{4f^{2}g^{2}+C^{2}}

(277)

Now the equation for
fg
becomes closed:

\frac{d}{d\tau}(fg)=\sqrt{4f^{2}g^{2}+C^{2}}(A-Bfg)

(278)

Let
h=fg

\frac{d}{d\tau}h=\sqrt{4h^{2}+C^{2}}(A-Bh)

(279)

Note that for this self-contained equation, unless
C=0
, it shall have only one attractive fixed point which is
h^{*}=A/B
. Thus asymptotically, it will always converge to the correct value.

When we face the weight tying case where
C=0
, the equation becomes

\frac{d}{d\tau}h=2|h|(A-Bh)

(280)

It will have another fixed point at
h=0
, which makes it impossible to converge to the fixed point
A/B
if the initialization
h(0)
has the opposite sign.

Figure 28:
Phase diagram for the simplified 2D dynamic system
. Above:

\frac{d}{d\tau}f=Ag-Bg^{2}f,\frac{d}{d\tau}g=Af-Bf^{2}g
for
A=1,B=2
.
We can see the manifold of stable attractors in the 1st and 3rd quadrants:

fg=A/B
, and the conserved quantity along the hyperbolic lines.

F.3
General deep linear network

Weight tying assumption / initialization

The weight tying deep network can be regarded as a special case.
The key gradient equation is

\nabla_{\Lambda_{l}}\mathcal{L}=[-\Lambda+(\sigma^{2}+\Lambda)\Lambda_{l}\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k}]\prod_{k=1,k\neq l}^{L}\Lambda_{k}

(281)

Let us assume all
\Lambda_{l}
are equal at initialization
3
3

3

\Lambda_{l}
is not to be confused with
\Lambda
, which is the spectrum of data
. Then

\nabla_{\Lambda_{l}}\mathcal{L}=[-\Lambda+(\sigma^{2}+\Lambda)\Lambda_{l}^{L}]\Lambda_{l}^{L-1}

(282)

Consider a single element of the diagonal at mode
k
,
a_{k}^{(l)}:=\Lambda_{l,k}
, then we have

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial a_{k}^{(l)}}=[-\lambda_{k}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\ \big(a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L}]\big(a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L-1}

(283)

Further consider the aggregated effect
c_{k}=\prod_{l}a_{k}^{(l)}
. Assuming weight tying, the gradient flow of this variable reads

\displaystyle\frac{\partial c_{k}}{\partial\tau}

\displaystyle=L\big(a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L-1}\frac{\partial a_{k}^{(l)}}{\partial\tau}

\displaystyle=-\eta L\big(a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L-1}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial a_{k}^{(l)}}

\displaystyle=-\eta L\ [-\lambda_{k}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\ \big(a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L}]\ \big(a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{2L-2}

\displaystyle=-\eta L\ [-\lambda_{k}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}]\ c_{k}^{\frac{2L-2}{L}}

\displaystyle=-\eta L\ [-\lambda_{k}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}]\ c_{k}^{2-\frac{2}{L}}

We arrive at the dynamics of the weights along eigenmode:

\frac{\partial c_{k}}{\partial\tau}=\eta L[\lambda_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}]c_{k}^{2-\frac{2}{L}}

(284)

Depth one and two cases

Note this form also encompasses the solution of one-layer and two-layer symmetric linear models by setting
L=1,2
.

General depth case

For general
L
, there is no closed-form solution; one only has an implicit solution to
c_{k}
involving the hypergeometric function
{}_{2}F_{1}
:

\Bigl(\frac{A\,c(\tau)}{B}\Bigr)^{2/L}{}_{2}F_{1}\Bigl(1,\frac{2}{L};1+\frac{2}{L};\frac{A\,c(\tau)}{B}\Bigr)\;=\;\Bigl(\frac{A\,c(0)}{B}\Bigr)^{2/L}{}_{2}F_{1}\Bigl(1,\frac{2}{L};1+\frac{2}{L};\frac{A\,c(0)}{B}\Bigr)\,e^{-\eta LB\tau}.

(285)

with substitution

A=(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}),B=\lambda_{k}
.

Infinite depth limit

The limiting case is
L\to\infty
, then the dynamics read

\displaystyle\frac{\partial c_{k}}{\partial\tau}

\displaystyle=-\eta L\ [-\lambda_{k}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}]\ c_{k}^{2-\frac{2}{L}}

\displaystyle(L\to\infty)

\displaystyle=\eta L\ [\lambda_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}]\ c_{k}^{2}

(286)

Then we have

\displaystyle\frac{dc_{k}}{[\lambda_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}]c_{k}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\eta L\ d\tau

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\ln\frac{c_{k}}{\lambda_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}c_{k}}

\displaystyle=\eta L\tau+C.

Setting the initial values as
c_{k}^{(0)}
, we have the implicit solution of
c_{k}
:

\frac{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\ln\frac{c_{k}}{\lambda_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}c_{k}}=\eta L\tau+\frac{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\ln\frac{c_{k}^{(0)}}{\lambda_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}^{(0)}}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}c_{k}^{(0)}}

where training time
\tau
can be expressed as a function of
c_{k}
:

\tau=\frac{1}{\eta L}\Big[\frac{\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\ln\frac{c_{k}\big(\lambda_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}^{(0)}\big)}{c_{k}^{(0)}\big(\lambda_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}\big)}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}c_{k}}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}c_{k}^{(0)}}\Big]

(287)

F.3.1
Deep Residual network

If the network is parametrized with a residual connection at each layer, i.e.,

\mathbf{W}=\prod_{i}(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{W}_{i})=(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{W}_{L})(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{W}_{L-1})...(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{W}_{1})
,
then it is a linear shift in parametrization. Using the same derivation and notation, assuming aligned initialization and homogeneous initialization, we can see

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial a_{k}^{(l)}}=\Big[-\lambda_{k}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\ \big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L}\Big]\ \big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L-1}

(288)

Let the overall effective weight be
c_{k}=\big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L}
, we have the Jacobian:

\frac{\partial c_{k}}{\partial a_{k}^{(l)}}\ =\ L\ \big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L-1}

Thus the dynamics of the effective weight is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial c_{k}}{\partial\tau}

\displaystyle=L\big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L-1}\frac{\partial a_{k}^{(l)}}{\partial\tau}

\displaystyle=-\eta L\big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L-1}\nabla_{a_{k}^{(l)}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-\eta L\big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L-1}\ \Big[-\lambda_{k}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\ \big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L}\Big]\ \big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L-1}

\displaystyle=-\eta L\ \Big[-\lambda_{k}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\ \big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L}\Big]\ \big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{2L-2}

\displaystyle=-\eta L\ \Big[-\lambda_{k}+(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})\ c_{k}\ \Big]\ c_{k}^{2-\frac{2}{L}}

(289)

We recover the same dynamics as the normal deep linear network:

\frac{\partial c_{k}}{\partial\tau}=\eta L[\lambda_{k}-(\sigma^{2}+\lambda_{k})c_{k}]c_{k}^{2-\frac{2}{L}}

(290)

Here the initialization is
c_{k}(0)=\big(1+a_{k}^{(l)}\big)^{L}
.

Thus in linear networks, residual connections do not change the learning dynamics, but just shift the initialization of weights.

Appendix G
Detailed Derivations for Linear Convolutional Network

In this section, we study a linear convolutional denoiser.
For simplicity, we focus on the one-dimensional case with a 1D convolutional filter
\mathbf{w}
. We omit the bias term and assume the input has zero mean, i.e.,
\mu=0
.
The convolutional denoiser is defined as

\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{x};\sigma)=\mathbf{w}_{\sigma}*\mathbf{x}

(291)

This convolution
*
can be equivalently represented as matrix multiplication, where the weight matrix
\mathbf{W}_{\sigma}
is a Toeplitz or circulant (when circular boundary condition).

\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{x};\sigma)=\mathbf{W}_{\sigma}\mathbf{x}

(292)

In this case, the score learning problem becomes a
circulant or Toeplitz regression
problem, which has been studied before
[
54
,
10
,
40
]
. This problem is similar to finding the best convolutional or deconvolutional kernel to a 1d sequence.

G.1
General set up

Cyclic weight matrix and spectral representation

Let’s consider the circular convolution case, where we ignore the boundary effect,
and assume the matrix
\mathbf{W}(\sigma)
is cyclic at any noise scale.
Since it’s cyclic, they can be diagonalized by discrete Fourier transform (DFT).

Let’s diagonalize the weight matrix with the DFT matrix,

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=F\Gamma(\sigma)F^{*}

(293)

specifically the DFT matrix is defined as

F_{mk}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\exp\left(-2\pi i\frac{mk}{N}\right),\quad m,k=0,1,2,\ldots,N-1

(294)

G.2
General analysis of sampling dynamics

Note that, if we assume weight matrix at every noise scale is circulant, then we always can solve the sampling dynamics on the Fourier basis, mode by mode. At its core, this is because all circulant matrices commute.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\sigma}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=-\frac{F\Gamma(\sigma)F^{*}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}}{\sigma}

\displaystyle F^{*}\frac{d}{d\sigma}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Gamma(\sigma)F^{*}\mathbf{x}-F^{*}\mathbf{x}}{\sigma}
