\displaystyle=-\frac{\Gamma(\sigma)-I}{\sigma}F^{*}\mathbf{x}

We can perform integration mode by mode.
Let
c_{k}=(F^{*}\mathbf{x})_{k}
,
\mathbf{x}=F\mathbf{c}

\frac{d}{d\sigma}c_{k}=-\frac{\gamma_{k}(\sigma)-1}{\sigma}c_{k}

(295)

Thus, if we know the Fourier parametrization of weights
\Gamma(\sigma)
, we can integrate the sampling of
\mathbf{x}
in closed form. We will need to solve these Fourier modes
\Gamma(\sigma)
of the weights during training dynamics. Integrating the ODE, we get

\Phi_{k}(\sigma)=\exp\Big(\int-\frac{\gamma_{k}(\lambda)-1}{\lambda}d\lambda\Big)

(296)

c_{k}(\sigma_{0})=\frac{\Phi_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{\Phi_{k}(\sigma_{T})}c_{k}(\sigma_{T})

(297)

Then the generated variance will be

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{T}^{2}\left(\frac{\Phi_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{\Phi_{k}(\sigma_{T})}\right)^{2}

(298)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=F\ \mbox{diag}(\tilde{\lambda}_{k})\ F^{*}

(299)

Because of this we can easily prove Proposition
5.2
(quoted from the main paper)

Proposition G.1
.

Linear convolutional denoisers with circular boundary can only model stationary Gaussian processes (GP), with independent Fourier modes, proof in App.
G.2
.

Proof.

Generated distributions from linear denoisers are Gaussian distributions, with covariance
\tilde{\Sigma}
.
Since the generated covariance is diagonalized by discrete Fourier transform
F
,
\tilde{\Sigma}
is circulant i.e. translation invariant. Given that the generated
\mathbf{x}
is Gaussian distributed,
\mathbf{x}
conforms to a stationary Gaussian process. While the integration function
\Phi_{k}(\sigma)
determines the covariance kernel of this Gaussian process.
∎

G.3
Full width linear convolutional network

G.3.1
Training dynamics of full width linear convolutional network

Gradient structure in spectral basis

Using the Fourier representation of the circulant weight

\mathbf{W}(\sigma)=F\mathbf{\Gamma}(\sigma)F^{*}

we can derive the gradient to the Fourier parameters
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\sigma)
which is a diagonal matrix.

Recall that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2\mathbf{b}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-2\Sigma+2\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)

Then the gradient to the Fourier parametrization reads

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L},d\mathbf{W}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L},Fd\mathbf{\Gamma}(\sigma)F^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle F^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}F^{-1*},d\mathbf{\Gamma}(\sigma)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle F^{*}\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}F,d\mathbf{\Gamma}(\sigma)\rangle

(300)

Thus

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=F^{*}\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}F

\displaystyle=-2F^{*}\Sigma F+2F^{*}\mathbf{W}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)F

\displaystyle=-2F^{*}\Sigma F+2F^{*}F\mathbf{\Gamma}F^{*}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)F

\displaystyle=-2F^{*}\Sigma F+2\mathbf{\Gamma}(\sigma^{2}I+F^{*}\Sigma F)

(301)

Under circular convolution assumption,
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a diagonal matrix,
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{ij}=\delta_{ij}\gamma_{i}
then

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\gamma_{i}}

\displaystyle=[\nabla_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\mathcal{L}]_{ii}

\displaystyle=-2[F^{*}\Sigma F]_{ii}+2\big[\mathbf{\Gamma}(\sigma^{2}I+F^{*}\Sigma F)\big]_{ii}

\displaystyle=-2[F^{*}\Sigma F]_{ii}+2\big[\delta_{ij}\gamma_{i}(\sigma^{2}I+F^{*}\Sigma F)\big]_{ii}

\displaystyle=-2[F^{*}\Sigma F]_{ii}+2\gamma_{i}\big(\sigma^{2}+[F^{*}\Sigma F]_{ii}\big)

(302)

The key entity is the
F^{*}\Sigma F
matrix, let’s define it as
\tilde{\Sigma}

Interpretation of
F^{*}\Sigma F

•

We can regard it as covariance matrix in the spectral basis, i.e.
covariance matrix of the complex variable
F^{*}\bar{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
.

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle=\mbox{cov}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbb{E}[\bar{\mathbf{x}}\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}]

\displaystyle F^{*}\Sigma F

\displaystyle=F^{*}\mathbb{E}[\bar{\mathbf{x}}\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}]F

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[F^{*}\bar{\mathbf{x}}\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}F]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[F^{*}\bar{\mathbf{x}}\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}F^{\top}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(F^{*}\bar{\mathbf{x}})(F^{*}\bar{\mathbf{x}})^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=\mbox{cov}(F^{*}\mathbf{x})

(303)

•

Diagonal values tell us about the power spectrum of the data.

[F^{*}\Sigma F]_{ii}=\mbox{Var}([F^{*}\bar{\mathbf{x}}]_{i})

(304)

\displaystyle F_{jk}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}e^{-2\pi i\,jk/N}

\displaystyle{}[F^{*}\Sigma F]_{jk}

\displaystyle=\sum_{mn}F_{jm}^{*}\Sigma_{mn}F_{nk}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{mn}\Sigma_{mn}e^{+2\pi i\,jm/N}e^{-2\pi i\,nk/N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{mn}\Sigma_{mn}e^{2\pi i\,\frac{jm-kn}{N}}

\displaystyle{}[F^{*}\Sigma F]_{jj}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{mn}\Sigma_{mn}e^{2\pi i\frac{j(m-n)}{N}}

Gradient flow on spectral parameter

Given the gradient structure,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\gamma_{k}}

\displaystyle=-2[F^{*}\Sigma F]_{kk}+2\gamma_{k}\big(\sigma^{2}+[F^{*}\Sigma F]_{kk}\big)

(305)

\displaystyle=-2\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}+2\gamma_{k}\big(\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}\big)

(306)

if we
directly perform gradient descent
on the
\gamma_{k}
variable, we have

\frac{d}{d\tau}\gamma_{k}=-\eta\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\gamma_{k}}

(307)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\gamma_{k}

\displaystyle=-\eta\Big[-2\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}+2\gamma_{k}\big(\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=2\eta\Big[\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}-\gamma_{k}\big(\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}\big)\Big]

(308)

Fixed point

Fixed point of the gradient flow is

\gamma_{k}^{*}=\frac{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}

(309)

Learning dynamics of Fourier modes

This is basically a first order dynamics ODE

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}+\Bigl(\gamma_{k}(0)-\frac{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}\Bigr)\ e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk})\tau}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{k}^{*}+\big(\gamma_{k}(0)-\gamma_{k}^{*}\big)e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk})\tau}

(310)

Remarks

•

This is exactly the same story as the one layer linear case, where

\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}
determines the convergence speed
per mode.

•

Spectral modes
\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}
with higher power will converge
faster, which usually corresponds to the lower spatial frequency modes.

•

Higher noise level will converge faster

Learning dynamics of weight entry as rotated Fourier mode dynamics

Lemma G.2

(Convolution Spectrum-entry relation)
.

The relationship between spectral and entry parametrization of filter weights is

w_{\ell}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}e^{2\pi i\,\frac{k\ell}{N}}\gamma_{k},\qquad\ell=0,\dots,N-1

(311)

Proof.

Derivation

Using our previous convention

\mathbf{W}_{ij}=w_{j-i}

Let the vector of the first row in
\mathbf{W}
be

\mathbf{W}_{0k}=w_{k}

Let the vector parameter be

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=[w_{0},w_{1},...,w_{N-1}]^{\top}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{W}_{0k})^{\top}

Then the connection between
\gamma_{k}
and weights
w_{\ell}
are
the following

\mathbf{W}=F\mathbf{\Gamma}F^{*}

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{jk}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n}F_{jm}\Gamma_{mn}F_{nk}^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\gamma_{m}F_{jm}F_{mk}^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\gamma_{m}F_{jm}F_{mk}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m}\gamma_{m}e^{-2\pi i\,jm/N}e^{+2\pi i\,mk/N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m}\gamma_{m}e^{2\pi i\frac{(k-j)m}{N}}

Thus

\displaystyle w_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}_{j,j+k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m}e^{+2\pi i\frac{km}{N}}\gamma_{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{m}F_{km}^{*}\gamma_{m}

∎

In vector notation, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}F^{*}\bm{\gamma}

(312)

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{N}F\mathbf{w}

(313)

Thus, they are related by a
\sqrt{N}
scaling and a unitary transform.

Similarly, the gradient to
\gamma
and that of
\mathbf{w}
has following relation

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{N}F^{-\top}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{N}F^{*}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}\mathcal{L}

(314)

Proof.

Derivation

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}\mathcal{L},d\bm{\gamma}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}\mathcal{L},\sqrt{N}Fd\mathbf{w}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\sqrt{N}F^{-\top}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}\mathcal{L},d\mathbf{w}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}\mathcal{L},d\mathbf{w}\rangle

∎

If we let the optimization parameter be the filters
\mathbf{w}
,
then we have gradient flow in
\mathbf{w}
space

\frac{d}{d\tau}\mathbf{w}=-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}\mathcal{L}

The corresponding gradient flow of
\bm{\gamma}
is

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}F^{*}\bm{\gamma}

\displaystyle=-\eta\sqrt{N}F^{*}\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\bm{\gamma}

\displaystyle=-\eta N\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}\mathcal{L}

So gradient flow in
\mathbf{w}
space is equivalent to gradient
flow in
\bm{\gamma}
, but scaling learning rate by
\eta\to N\eta
!

Conversely if we treat
\bm{\gamma}
as optimization parameter and use
gradient flow, then it’s equivalent to scale learning rate
\eta\to\eta/N
.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\bm{\gamma}

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla_{\bm{\gamma}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\sqrt{N}F\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=-\eta\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}F\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\eta}{N}\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}\mathcal{L}

Remarks

•

Thus we can solve gradient flow in any representation, but translating
to solution of the other variable just by rescaling the learning rate.

•

This trick only works when the kernel width is
N
, or the spectral
parameter will be constrained in a subspace, which will alter its
dynamics.

•

Direct spectral parametrization without any constraint is basically
equivalent to the entry wise parametrization. It does not provide
extra inductive bias.

Corollary G.3
.

For linear circular convolutional denoiser,
\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{x},\sigma)=\mathbf{w}*\mathbf{x}
,
the solution to gradient flow on filter weight
\mathbf{w}
is
\mathbf{w}(\tau,\sigma)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}F^{*}\bm{\gamma}(\tau,\sigma)
,
where the spectral parameter of
k
-th Fourier mode evolves as

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}+\Bigl(\gamma_{k}(0,\sigma)-\frac{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}\Bigr)\ e^{-2N\eta(\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk})\tau}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{k}^{*}(\sigma)+\big(\gamma_{k}(0,\sigma)-\gamma_{k}^{*}(\sigma)\big)e^{-2N\eta(\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk})\tau}

(315)

where
\gamma_{k}^{*}(\sigma)=\frac{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}
,
and
\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}
is the variance of Fourier mode.

G.3.2
Sampling dynamics of full width linear convolutional network

Let’s project the sampling equation on Fourier modes,

F^{*}\frac{d}{d\sigma}\mathbf{x}=-\frac{\Gamma(\sigma)-I}{\sigma}F^{*}\mathbf{x}

(316)

Let
c_{k}=(F^{*}\mathbf{x})_{k}
,
\mathbf{x}=F\mathbf{c}
,

\frac{d}{d\sigma}c_{k}=-\frac{\gamma_{k}(\sigma)-1}{\sigma}c_{k}

(317)

The variance amplification factor is expressed through the integral,

\Phi_{k}(\sigma)=\exp\left(\int-\frac{\gamma_{k}(\lambda)-1}{\lambda}d\lambda\right)

(318)

Generated distribution after convergence

For the converged denoiser we have
\gamma_{k}^{*}=\frac{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}

\displaystyle\Phi_{k}(\sigma)=

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\int_{\epsilon}^{\sigma}-\frac{\gamma_{k}(\lambda)-1}{\lambda}d\lambda\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\int_{\epsilon}^{\sigma}-\frac{\frac{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\lambda^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}-1}{\lambda}d\lambda\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\int_{\epsilon}^{\sigma}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}d\lambda\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}\ln(\lambda^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk})|_{\lambda=\epsilon}^{\lambda=\sigma}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\epsilon^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}}

(319)

Variance of the learned distribution

\tilde{\lambda_{k}}=\sigma_{T}^{2}\Big(\frac{\Phi_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{\Phi_{k}(\sigma_{T})}\Big)^{2}=\sigma_{T}^{2}\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\sigma_{T}^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}\approx\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}

(320)

The generated covariance will be

\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=F\mbox{diag}(\tilde{\lambda_{k}})F^{*}

(321)

\displaystyle\approx F\mbox{diag}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk})F^{*}

Remarks

•

Basically it’s the same story as the one-layer linear network, the
major difference is, instead of evolution along the eigenbasis of
data covariance (PCs), the parametrization of convolutional network
enforces the learning dynamics to align with the Fourier basis regardless
of the original PC of the data. It treats the data as if it’s stationary
/ translation invariant.

•

The generated data will be independent along each Fourier mode, just
like the fully connected case, the generated data is independent along
each eigen-mode.

•

This amounts to decorrelating the original covariance in the spectral
domain, making it translation invariant / circulant.

Generated distribution during training

Utilizing the gradient flow solution under
spectral parametrization
, we have for each Fourier mode:

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(\tau;\sigma)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{k}^{*}+\big(\gamma_{k}(0;\sigma)-\gamma_{k}^{*}\big)e^{-2\eta(\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk})\tau}

We can solve the sampling ODE during training.
Assume the initial value of each Fourier mode is the same across noise scale
\gamma_{k}(0;\sigma)=\gamma_{k}(0),\ \forall\sigma
.
We can write down the generated variance through training time as,

4
4

4

One thing to note is that it’s highly likely that the initial value
of
\gamma_{k}(0;\sigma)
can differ for each Fourier mode, i.e.
different Fourier modes are initialized at different weights due to
the filter initialization.

\displaystyle\Phi_{k}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\int^{\sigma}-\frac{\gamma_{k}(\lambda)-1}{\lambda}d\lambda\Big)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\int^{\sigma}-\frac{\frac{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\lambda^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}+\big(\gamma_{k}(0)-\frac{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}{\lambda^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}\big)e^{-2\eta(\lambda^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk})\tau}-1}{\lambda}d\lambda\Big)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}+\sigma^{2}}\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\bigg[(1-\gamma_{k}(0))\,\mbox{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\sigma^{2}\right)e^{-2\eta\tau\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}-\,\mbox{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\left(\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}\right)\right)\bigg]\bigg)

(322)

Using the solution to gradient flow of
filter weights parametrization
, we effectively
amplify the learning rate
\eta\to N\eta
in the expression.

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(\tau;\sigma)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{k}^{*}+\big(\gamma_{k}(0;\sigma)-\gamma_{k}^{*}\big)e^{-2N\eta(\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk})\tau}

(323)

The generated distribution has variance
\breve{\Sigma}=F\breve{\Lambda}F^{*}
, where
\breve{\Lambda}_{kk}=\sigma_{T}^{2}\Big(\frac{\Phi_{k}(\sigma_{0})}{\Phi_{k}(\sigma_{T})}\Big)^{2}

controls variance along Fourier modes.

\Phi_{k}(\sigma)=\sqrt{\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}+\sigma^{2}}\exp\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\bigg[(1-\gamma_{k}(0))\,\mbox{Ei}\left(-2N\eta\tau\sigma^{2}\right)e^{-2N\eta\tau\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}}-\,\mbox{Ei}\left(-2N\eta\tau\left(\sigma^{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{kk}\right)\right)\bigg]\bigg)

(324)

G.4
Local patch linear convolutional network

Now, let’s consider a local filter
\mathbf{w}
.

Spectral representation and spatial locality constraint

The major difference is that if we have a locality constraint in
\mathbf{w}
,
the corresponding constraint in
\gamma
will be constrained in a
lower-dimensional space:

w_{\ell}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}e^{2\pi i\,\frac{kl}{N}}\gamma_{k}

(325)

\displaystyle\mathbf{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{N}F\mathbf{w}

(326)

\displaystyle\gamma_{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}e^{-2\pi i\,\frac{kl}{N}}w_{k}

(327)

But if
w_{k}=0,\ \forall k\geq K
, then the spectral sum is only over
K
:

\gamma_{l}=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}e^{-2\pi i\,\frac{kl}{N}}w_{k}

(328)

Specific case: kernel 3 convolution

Consider the case where the kernel size is 3, so only
w_{1},w_{0},w_{-1}\neq 0
.
Then the spectrum can only have a DC and a cosine and sine component:

\displaystyle\gamma_{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\{-1,0,1\}}e^{-2\pi i\,\frac{kl}{N}}w_{k}

\displaystyle=w_{0}+w_{1}e^{-2\pi i\,\frac{l}{N}}+w_{-1}e^{2\pi i\,\frac{l}{N}}

\displaystyle=w_{0}+(w_{1}+w_{-1})\cos\left(\frac{2\pi l}{N}\right)+i(w_{-1}-w_{1})\sin\left(\frac{2\pi l}{N}\right)

(329)

Basically, the small kernel size mandates that its spectrum
\gamma
cannot vary very quickly! Thus, the spectral representation
has to be very smooth: just sine and cosine waves.

G.4.1
Training dynamics of patch linear convolutional net

To study the training dynamics of the linear local convolutional network, it’s easier to directly work with the entries of the filters.

Consider the Toeplitz weight matrix (general boundary condition):

\mathbf{W}=\begin{pmatrix}w_{0}&w_{1}&w_{2}&\cdots&w_{d-1}\\
w_{-1}&w_{0}&w_{1}&\cdots&w_{d-2}\\
w_{-2}&w_{-1}&w_{0}&\cdots&w_{d-3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
w_{-d+1}&w_{-d+2}&w_{-d+3}&\cdots&w_{0}\end{pmatrix}

(330)

Shift matrix formulation

Generally, a Toeplitz matrix can be written as

\mathbf{W}=\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}w_{k}P^{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}w_{-k}(P^{\top})^{k}

(331)

where the (non-circulant) shift matrix is
P
,

P=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{bmatrix}

(332)

This representation compactly expresses the weight matrix as a weighted
sum of shifted matrix powers, enabling us to write down the gradient efficiently.

Remarks

•

For circulant shift matrix,
P^{\top}=P^{-1}
.
P^{\top}P=0
exactly.

•

For non-circulant shift matrix, approximately
P^{\top}=P^{-1}
but
there will be issues at the boundary condition.

•

Taking the trace with it also allows us to elegantly extract entries from a matrix.

•

Multiplying by this shift matrix allows us to shift the matrix

This decomposition of weights allows us to write down the loss and
the gradient.

For
k\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial w_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j-i=k}G_{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j-i=k}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\mathbf{W}_{ij}}

\displaystyle=\langle P^{k},G\rangle

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[(P^{k})^{\top}G]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[(P^{k})^{\top}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\mathbf{W}}]

(333)

For
k<0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial w_{-k}}

\displaystyle=\langle(P^{\top})^{k},G\rangle

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[P^{k}G]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[P^{k}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\mathbf{W}}]

(334)

Using this formulation, we can express the gradient with respect to each entry:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial w_{k}}

\displaystyle=2\operatorname{Tr}[-(P^{k})^{\top}\Sigma+(P^{k})^{\top}W(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)]

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Tr}[(P^{k})^{\top}\Sigma]+2\operatorname{Tr}[(P^{k})^{\top}W(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)]

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Tr}[(P^{k})^{\top}\Sigma]+2\operatorname{Tr}\left[(P^{k})^{\top}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{d-1}w_{m}P^{m}+\sum_{m=1}^{d-1}w_{-m}(P^{\top})^{m}\right)(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)\right]

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Tr}[(P^{k})^{\top}\Sigma]+2\operatorname{Tr}\left[\left(\sum_{m=0}^{d-1}w_{m}(P^{k})^{\top}P^{m}+\sum_{m=1}^{d-1}w_{-m}(P^{\top})^{k+m}\right)(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)\right]

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Tr}[(P^{k})^{\top}\Sigma]+2\sum_{m=0}^{d-1}w_{m}\operatorname{Tr}[(P^{k})^{\top}P^{m}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)]+2\sum_{m=1}^{d-1}w_{-m}\operatorname{Tr}[(P^{\top})^{k+m}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)]

(335)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial w_{-k}}

\displaystyle=2\operatorname{Tr}[-P^{k}\Sigma+P^{k}W(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)]

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Tr}[P^{k}\Sigma]+2\operatorname{Tr}[P^{k}W(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)]

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Tr}[P^{k}\Sigma]+2\operatorname{Tr}\left[P^{k}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{d-1}w_{m}P^{m}+\sum_{m=1}^{d-1}w_{-m}(P^{\top})^{m}\right)(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)\right]

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Tr}[P^{k}\Sigma]+2\operatorname{Tr}\left[\left(\sum_{m=0}^{d-1}w_{m}P^{k+m}+\sum_{m=1}^{d-1}w_{-m}P^{k}(P^{\top})^{m}\right)(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)\right]

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Tr}[P^{k}\Sigma]+2\sum_{m=0}^{d-1}w_{m}\operatorname{Tr}[P^{k+m}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)]+2\sum_{m=1}^{d-1}w_{-m}\operatorname{Tr}[P^{k}(P^{\top})^{m}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)]

(336)

We denote the general
k
-shift operator (non-circulant boundary condition) as

S(k)=\begin{cases}P^{k}&k\geq 0\\
(P^{\top})^{-k}&k<0\end{cases}

(337)

and

S(k)^{\top}=(P^{k})^{\top}=(P^{\top})^{k}=S(-k)

(338)

Define a special function with two indices:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}[k,m]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[S(m)(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)S(k)^{\top}]

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\operatorname{Tr}[S(m)S(k)^{\top}]+\operatorname{Tr}[S(m)\Sigma S(k)^{\top}]

(339)

The first term is diagonal, the second term is not. The non-diagonal terms
all come from the second term.

Note that this special function is symmetric, which is understandable,
since it will be functioning as the new covariance matrix:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}[m,k]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[S(m)(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)S(k)^{\top}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[S(k)(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)S(m)^{\top}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}[k,m]

(340)

Then the gradient can be written in this simplified form, similar to before:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial w_{k}}

\displaystyle=-2\operatorname{Tr}[S(k)^{\top}\Sigma]+2\sum_{m=1-d}^{d-1}\mathcal{T}[k,m]w_{m}

(341)

Thus, to study the learning dynamics of convolutional linear models,
the object that needs to be focused on is
\mathcal{T}[k,m]
, the spectrum
of it. Hence, the learning dynamics of
w
will move first along higher
eigenvalues of
\mathcal{T}
, then along lower eigenvalues.

Interpretation of
\mathcal{T}
matrix

Note that the
\mathcal{T}[k,m]
matrix can be regarded as the spatially
averaged version of the covariance matrix, in particular by averaging local cross
covariances of pixels at distances
k,m
.

Optimal solution

The fixed point of the equation is the following linear equation:

\sum_{m}\mathcal{T}[k,m]w_{m}=\operatorname{Tr}[S(k)\Sigma]

(342)

Let the vector
R_{k}=\operatorname{Tr}[S(k)\Sigma]
,
k\in\{1-d,\ldots,d-1\}
,

w^{*}=\mathcal{T}^{-1}R

(343)

Here we have successfully derived the matrix equation for gradient flow
of weights
w
.

Cyclic case (circular convolution)

Now consider the cyclic shift matrix:

P=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1\\
1&0&0&\cdots&0\end{bmatrix}

(344)

Then
P^{\top}=P^{-1}
, so we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}[k,m]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[S(m)(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)S(k)^{\top}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[P^{m}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)P^{-k}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[P^{m-k}(\sigma^{2}I+\Sigma)]

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\operatorname{Tr}[P^{m-k}]+\operatorname{Tr}[P^{m-k}\Sigma]

(345)

Note that

\operatorname{Tr}[P^{m-k}]=N\delta_{mk}

(346)

So,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}[k,m]

\displaystyle=N\sigma^{2}\delta_{mk}+\operatorname{Tr}[P^{m-k}\Sigma]

\displaystyle=N\left(\sigma^{2}\delta_{mk}+\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}[P^{m-k}\Sigma]\right)

(347)

Note that the second term is the shift-averaged version of the covariance
matrix:

\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}[P^{m-k}\Sigma]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Sigma_{i,i+(m-k)\bmod N}

(348)

Let’s define a new averaged cross-covariance vector:

\displaystyle\chi[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}[P^{k}\Sigma]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Sigma_{i,i+k\bmod N}

(349)

Note
\chi[k]=\chi[-k]
. Define:

\displaystyle R_{k}

\displaystyle=N\chi[k]

(350)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}[k,m]

\displaystyle=N\left(\sigma^{2}\delta_{mk}+\chi[m-k]\right)

(351)

We want to solve the linear system:

\sum_{m}\mathcal{T}[k,m]w_{m}-R_{k}=0

(352)

Note that
\mathcal{T}[k,m]
is a Toeplitz matrix.

In the circulant case, the optimal solution satisfies

\sum_{m=-K}^{K}\left(N\sigma^{2}\delta_{km}+R_{k-m}\right)w^{*}_{m}=R_{k}

(353)

where
R_{k}
is the averaged version of the covariance matrix. So again,
it is a circulant regression with a Ridge-like penalty:

R_{k}=\operatorname{Tr}[P^{k}\Sigma]

(354)

If we let
\breve{\Sigma}_{k,m}=\frac{1}{N}R_{k-m}\in\mathbb{R}^{2K+1\times 2K+1}

be the Toeplitz patch covariance matrix, then we can write
R_{k}

as the center column of it:

\sum_{m=-K}^{K}\left(N\sigma^{2}\delta_{km}+N\breve{\Sigma}_{k,m}\right)w_{m}^{*}=N\breve{\Sigma}_{k,0}

(355)

The solution can be written in vector form as:

\displaystyle(N\sigma^{2}I+N\breve{\Sigma})w^{*}

\displaystyle=N\breve{\Sigma}_{:,0}

\displaystyle w^{*}

\displaystyle=(N\sigma^{2}I+N\breve{\Sigma})^{-1}N\breve{\Sigma}_{:,0}

\displaystyle=\left((\sigma^{2}I+\breve{\Sigma})^{-1}\breve{\Sigma}\right)_{:,0}

(356)

Thus, the denoiser equals the central column from the Gaussian solution
to the patch covariance.

The gradient flow dynamics is:

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial w_{k}}=-2R_{k}+2\sum_{m=1-d}^{d-1}(N\sigma^{2}\delta_{km}+R_{k-m})w_{m}

(357)

We can write the flow dynamics in vector form:

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathbf{w}}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=-\eta\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\mathbf{w}}

\displaystyle=2\eta\left(N\breve{\Sigma}_{:,0}-N(\sigma^{2}I+\breve{\Sigma})\mathbf{w}\right)

\displaystyle=2\eta N\left(\breve{\Sigma}_{:,0}-(\sigma^{2}I+\breve{\Sigma})\mathbf{w}\right)

(358)

The solution will be:

\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}^{*}+\exp\left(-2\eta N(\sigma^{2}I+\breve{\Sigma})\right)(\mathbf{w}(0)-\mathbf{w}^{*})

(359)

with
\mathbf{w}^{*}=\left((\sigma^{2}I+\breve{\Sigma})^{-1}\breve{\Sigma}\right)_{:,0}
.

G.4.2
Sampling dynamics of patch linear convolutional net

Consider a kernel of width
2K+1
:

\displaystyle\gamma_{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=-K}^{K}e^{-2\pi i\,\frac{kl}{N}}w_{k}

\displaystyle=w_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{k}e^{-2\pi i\,\frac{kl}{N}}+w_{-k}e^{2\pi i\,\frac{kl}{N}}

\displaystyle=w_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}(w_{k}+w_{-k})\cos\left(\frac{2\pi kl}{N}\right)+i(w_{-k}-w_{k})\sin\left(\frac{2\pi kl}{N}\right)

(360)

For symmetric filter weights
w_{k}=w_{-k}
, we have:

\gamma_{l}=w_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}2w_{k}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi kl}{N}\right)

(361)

During sampling, we have

\frac{d}{d\sigma}c_{k}=-\frac{\gamma_{k}(\sigma)-1}{\sigma}c_{k}-b_{k}(\sigma)

(362)

The key integral governing the variance is:

\displaystyle\Phi_{k}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int^{\sigma}-\frac{\gamma_{k}(\lambda)-1}{\lambda}d\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int^{\sigma}-\frac{w_{0}(\lambda)+\sum_{l=1}^{K}2w_{l}(\lambda)\cos\left(\frac{2\pi lk}{N}\right)-1}{\lambda}d\lambda\right)

(363)

Thus we can see it integrates these sinusoidal modulations in the frequency domain.

G.5
Appendix: Useful math

Discrete Fourier Transformation (DFT) matrix

F_{jk}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}e^{-2\pi i\,jk/N}

(364)

Property

Symmetry

F=F^{\top}

Conjugacy

FF^{*}=I

\displaystyle F_{jk}F_{km}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k}^{N}e^{-2\pi i\,jk/N}e^{+2\pi i\,km/N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k}^{N}e^{-2\pi i\,\frac{k(j-m)}{N}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{jm}

Properties of Circulant and DFT

Consider matrices of the following form

M=F\Lambda F^{*}

(365)

Explicitly, the matrix reads

\displaystyle M_{jk}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}F_{jm}\lambda_{m}F_{mk}^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\exp\left(-2\pi i\frac{jm}{N}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\exp\left(2\pi i\frac{mk}{N}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\exp\left(-2\pi i\frac{jm}{N}+2\pi i\frac{mk}{N}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\exp\left(-2\pi i\frac{m(j-k)}{N}\right)

Thus we see
M_{jk}
depends only on
j-k
, hence is circulant. Let’s
define the circulant coefficients
c_{j-k}:=M_{j,k}
.

Then we have for
\Delta=0,1,...N-1

c_{\Delta}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\exp\left(-2\pi i\frac{m\Delta}{N}\right)

(366)

The special case is the “DC” non-oscillating term, which are the
diagonal values in
M

c_{0}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}

the adjacent sub-diagonal values in
M
are

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\exp\left(-2\pi i\frac{m}{N}\right)

\displaystyle c_{N-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\exp\left(-2\pi i\frac{m(N-1)}{N}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m}\lambda_{m}\exp\left(+2\pi i\frac{m}{N}\right)

More generally
c_{k}=c_{N-k}^{*}
are complex conjugates, or equal
in the real case.

Properties of the
\Lambda
spectrum

Since
\Sigma
is real symmetric, the eigenvalues exhibit mirror,
i.e. even symmetry
\lambda_{k}=\lambda_{N-k}
for
k=1,2,...,N-1

There are one or two standalone eigenvalues: when
N
is odd,
\lambda_{0}

is the zero-th frequency, DC component, which is unpaired.

When
N
is even,
\lambda_{0}
(DC component) and
\lambda_{N//2}

(Nyquist frequency) are both standing alone, which are both unpaired.

Appendix H
Detailed derivation of Flow Matching model

Consider the objective of flow matching
[
39
]
, at a certain
t

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}),\ \mathbf{x}_{1}\sim p_{1}}\|u(\mathbf{x}_{t};t)-(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})\|^{2}

(367)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}),\ \mathbf{x}_{1}\sim p_{1}}\|u((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1};t)-(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})\|^{2}

(368)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=(1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1}

(369)

Given linear function approximator of the velocity field,

u(\mathbf{x};t)=\mathbf{W}_{t}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}_{t}

(370)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}),\ \mathbf{x}_{1}\sim p_{1}}\|\mathbf{W}_{t}((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})+\mathbf{b}_{t}-(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}),\ \mathbf{x}_{1}\sim p_{1}}\big(\mathbf{W}_{t}((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})+\mathbf{b}_{t}-(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})\big)^{\top}\big(\mathbf{W}_{t}((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})+\mathbf{b}_{t}-(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}),\ \mathbf{x}_{1}\sim p_{1}}\mbox{Tr}\bigg[((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{t}((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})+\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{b}+(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})^{\top}(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})

\displaystyle\quad-2(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})^{\top}\mathbf{b}_{t}-2(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{t}((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})+2\mathbf{b}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{t}((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}),\ \mathbf{x}_{1}\sim p_{1}}\mbox{Tr}\bigg[\mathbf{W}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{t}((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})^{\top}+\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{b}+(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})^{\top}(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})

\displaystyle\quad-2(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})^{\top}\mathbf{b}_{t}-2\mathbf{W}_{t}((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})^{\top}+2\mathbf{b}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{t}((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})\bigg]

Similar to the diffusion case, it will also depend only on the mean and covariance of
p_{1}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}}[(1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1}]

\displaystyle=t\mu

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}}[\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0}]

\displaystyle=\mu

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}}[(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})^{\top}(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}}[\mathbf{x}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{1}-2\mathbf{x}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{0}+\mathbf{x}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{0}]

\displaystyle=\mbox{Tr}[\bm{\Sigma}+\mu\mu^{\top}+\mathbf{I}]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}}[((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0})^{\top}]

\displaystyle=t(\bm{\Sigma}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\top})-(1-t)\mathbf{I}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}}[((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})((1-t)\mathbf{x}_{0}+t\mathbf{x}_{1})^{\top}]

\displaystyle=t^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\top})+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}

Taking full expectation, the average loss reads.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\mbox{Tr}\bigg[\mathbf{W}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{t}\big(t^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\top})+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\big)+\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{b}+(\bm{\Sigma}+\mu\mu^{\top}+\mathbf{I})

\displaystyle\quad-2\mu^{\top}\mathbf{b}_{t}-2\mathbf{W}_{t}\big(t(\bm{\Sigma}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\top})-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\big)+2t\mathbf{b}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{t}\mu\bigg]

(371)

The gradients with respect to parameters are

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2[\mathbf{b}-\mu+t\mathbf{W}\mu]

(372)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2\Bigl[\mathbf{W}\bigl(t^{2}(\bm{\Sigma}+\mu\mu^{\top})+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)-\bigl(t(\bm{\Sigma}+\mu\mu^{\top})-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\bigr)+t\mathbf{b}\mu^{\top}\Bigr],

(373)

Simplifying case
\mu=0

Note the special case
\mu=0

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2\mathbf{b}

(374)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2\Bigl[\mathbf{W}\bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)-\bigl(t\bm{\Sigma}-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\bigr)\Bigr],

(375)

Optimal solution

The optimal solution to the full case is

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mu-t\mathbf{W}^{*}\mu

(376)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}^{*}

\displaystyle=\big(t\bm{\Sigma}-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\big)\ \bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)^{-1}

(377)

We can represent it on the eigenbasis of
\bm{\Sigma}
,
[u_{1},...u_{d}]

\mathbf{W}^{*}=\sum_{k}\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

(378)

Asymptotics

Consider the limit
t\to 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{t\to 0}^{*}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{I}

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{t\to 0}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mu

Consider the limit
t\to 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{t\to 1}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{t\to 1}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mu-\mathbf{W}_{t\to 1}^{*}\mu=0

H.1
Solution to the flow matching sampling ODE with optimal linear solution

Solving the sampling ODE of flow matching integrating from 0 to 1, with the linear vector field

\frac{d\mathbf{x}}{dt}=u(\mathbf{x};t)

(379)

Under the linear solution case

\frac{d\mathbf{x}}{dt}=\mathbf{W}^{*}_{t}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}^{*}_{t}

(380)

Simplified zero mean case
\mu=0

\frac{d\mathbf{x}}{dt}=\sum_{k}\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}

Solving the flow-matching sampling ODE mode by mode

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\frac{d\mathbf{x}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\frac{dc_{k}(t)}{dt}

\displaystyle=\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}c_{k}(t)

\ln c_{k}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\ln\bigl|\;t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr|+C.

c_{k}(t)=C\sqrt{t^{2}\lambda_{k}\;+(1-t)^{2}}

(381)

\frac{c_{k}(t)}{c_{k}(0)}=\sqrt{t^{2}\lambda_{k}\;+(1-t)^{2}}

\frac{c_{k}(1)}{c_{k}(0)}=\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}

This is the correct scaling of
\mathbf{x}
. The sampling trajectory of
\mathbf{x_{t}}
reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}c_{k}(t)\mathbf{u}_{k}

(382)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sqrt{t^{2}\lambda_{k}\;+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{0}

(383)

Thus, at time
t
the covariance of the sampled points is

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}_{t}\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}]=\sum_{k}(t^{2}\lambda_{k}\;+(1-t)^{2})\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

(384)

with variance
\tilde{\lambda_{k}}=t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}
along eigenmode
\mathbf{u}_{k}

General case
\mu\neq 0

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathbf{x}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}_{t}^{*}\mathbf{x}+\mu-t\mathbf{W}_{t}^{*}\mu

\displaystyle=\mu+\sum_{k}\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-t\mu)

(385)

It can also be solved mode by mode. Redefine variable
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{x}_{t}-t\mu

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathbf{y}_{t}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\frac{d\mathbf{x}_{t}}{dt}-\mu

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}

Then each mode can be solved accordingly,
c_{k}(t)=\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}

\frac{dc_{k}(t)}{dt}=\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}c_{k}(t)

Using the same solution as above, we get the full solution for the sampling equation with any
\mu

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=t\mu+\sum_{k}c_{k}(t)\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle=t\mu+\sum_{k}\sqrt{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{0}

(386)

H.2
Learning dynamics of flow matching objective (single layer)

Simplifying case
\mu=0
, single layer network

Note the special case
\mu=0

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2\mathbf{b}

(387)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2\Bigl[\mathbf{W}\bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)-\bigl(t\bm{\Sigma}-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\bigr)\Bigr]

(388)

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathbf{W}}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}

(389)

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathbf{W}}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=-2\eta\Bigl[\mathbf{W}\bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)-\bigl(t\bm{\Sigma}-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\bigr)\Bigr]

(390)

Using the eigenbasis projection

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathbf{W}\mathbf{u}_{k}}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=-2\eta\Bigl[\mathbf{W}\bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)-\bigl(t\bm{\Sigma}-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\bigr)\Bigr]\mathbf{u}_{k}

(391)

\displaystyle=-2\eta\Bigl[\mathbf{W}\mathbf{u}_{k}\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)-\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)\mathbf{u}_{k}\Bigr]

(392)

\displaystyle=-2\eta\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\Bigl[\mathbf{W}\mathbf{u}_{k}-\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\Bigr]

(393)

\mathbf{W}(\tau)\mathbf{u}_{k}-\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}=A\exp\Big(-2\eta\tau\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\Big)

(394)

\mathbf{W}(\tau)\mathbf{u}_{k}=\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}+\Big(\mathbf{W}(0)\mathbf{u}_{k}-\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\Big)\exp\Big(-2\eta\tau\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\Big)

(395)

The full solutions of the weight and bias are

\mathbf{W}(\tau)=\mathbf{W}^{*}+\sum_{k}\Big(\mathbf{W}(0)\mathbf{u}_{k}-\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\Big)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\exp\Big(-2\eta\tau\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\Big)

(396)

\mathbf{b}(\tau)=\mathbf{b}(0)\exp(-2\eta\tau)

(397)

It is easy to see this solution has a similar structure to that of the denoising score matching objective for diffusion models.

Remarks

•

The learning dynamics of weight eigenmodes at different times
t
were visualized in Fig.
8
A
.

•

Note that the convergence speed of each mode is
\exp(-2\eta\tau\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr))
.
Similarly, at the same time
t
, the higher the data variance
\lambda_{k}
,
the faster the convergence speed.

•

At smaller
t
, all eigenmodes converge at similar speed.

•

At larger
t\sim 1
, the eigenmodes are resolved at distinct speeds
depending on the eigenvalues.

•

Note that for each eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}
, there is a special time point
where the convergence speed is maximized:
t^{*}=1/(\lambda_{k}+1)
.

H.2.1
Interaction of weight learning and flow sampling

Consider the sampling dynamics of a flow matching model with learned weights:

\frac{d\mathbf{x}}{dt}=\mathbf{W}(\tau,t)\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}(\tau,t)

(398)

Assume the weight initialization is aligned and the same across
t
:

\mathbf{W}(0,t)=\sum_{k}Q_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

(399)

then

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}(\tau,t)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}^{*}+\sum_{k}\Big(\mathbf{W}(0)-\mathbf{W}^{*}\Big)\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\exp\Big(-2\eta\tau\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\Big)

(400)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}+\sum_{k}\Big(Q_{k}-\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\Big)\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\exp\Big(-2\eta\tau\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\Big)

(401)

Ignoring the bias part, consider the weight integration along
c_{k}(t)=\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{x}(t)
:

\frac{d}{dt}c_{k}(t)=\left[\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}+\Big(Q_{k}-\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\Big)\exp\Big(-2\eta\tau\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\Big)\right]c_{k}(t)

(402)

We have the integration of the coefficient:

\displaystyle I=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dt\Big[\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}+\Big(Q_{k}-\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\Big)\exp\Big(-2\eta\tau\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}Q_{k}e^{-\frac{2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\left(\text{erf}\left(\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\frac{\eta\tau}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\right)+\text{erf}\left(\sqrt{2}\lambda_{k}\sqrt{\frac{\eta\tau}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\right)\right)}{4\sqrt{\eta\tau\left(\lambda_{k}+1\right)}}+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\text{Ei}(-2\eta\tau)-\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}\right)+\log\left(\lambda_{k}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\lambda_{k}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\text{Ei}(-2\eta\tau)-\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2\eta\tau\left(\lambda_{k}+1\right)}}Q_{k}e^{-\frac{2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+1}}

\displaystyle\left(\text{erf}\left(\sqrt{\frac{2\eta\tau}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\right)+\text{erf}\left(\lambda_{k}\sqrt{\frac{2\eta\tau}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\right)\right)

(403)

\displaystyle\Phi_{k}(1)

\displaystyle=\exp(I)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\ \int_{0}^{1}dt\Big[\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}+\Big(Q_{k}-\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\Big)\exp\Big(-2\eta\tau\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\Big)\Big]\ \Big)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\lambda_{k}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\text{Ei}(-2\eta\tau)-\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2\eta\tau\left(\lambda_{k}+1\right)}}Q_{k}e^{-\frac{2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+1}}

\displaystyle\quad\left(\text{erf}\left(\sqrt{\frac{2\eta\tau}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\right)+\text{erf}\left(\lambda_{k}\sqrt{\frac{2\eta\tau}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\right)\right)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}\sqrt{\exp\Big(\text{Ei}(-2\eta\tau)-\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}\right)\Big)}\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2\eta\tau\left(\lambda_{k}+1\right)}}Q_{k}e^{-\frac{2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+1}}

\displaystyle\quad\left(\text{erf}\left(\sqrt{\frac{2\eta\tau}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\right)+\text{erf}\left(\lambda_{k}\sqrt{\frac{2\eta\tau}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\right)\right)\Big)

(404)

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{k}}{\lambda_{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Phi_{k}(1)^{2}}{\lambda_{k}}

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\text{Ei}(-2\eta\tau)-\text{Ei}\left(-2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}\right)\Big)\times

(405)

\displaystyle\quad\exp\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2\eta\tau\left(\lambda_{k}+1\right)}}Q_{k}e^{-\frac{2\eta\tau\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\left(\text{erf}\left(\sqrt{\frac{2\eta\tau}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\right)+\text{erf}\left(\lambda_{k}\sqrt{\frac{2\eta\tau}{\lambda_{k}+1}}\right)\right)\Big)

Remarks

•

The learning dynamics of generated variance were visualized in Fig.
8
B
.

•

The power law relationship between the convergence time
\tau^{*}_{k}
of generated variance and the target variance
\lambda_{k}
was shown in Fig.
9
. For the harmonic mean criterion, the power law coefficient was also close to
-1
.

H.3
Learning dynamics of flow matching objective (two layers)

Let
\mathbf{W}=PP^{\top}
. Given the general loss,

\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}=2\Bigl[\mathbf{W}\bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)-\bigl(t\bm{\Sigma}-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\bigr)\Bigr]

(406)

\displaystyle\nabla_{P}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L})P+(\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L})^{\top}P

\displaystyle=\bigg[\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L}+(\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\mathcal{L})^{\top}\bigg]P

\displaystyle\nabla_{P}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2\Bigl[PP^{\top}\bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)-\bigl(t\bm{\Sigma}-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\bigr)\Bigr]P

\displaystyle\quad+2\Bigl[\bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)PP^{\top}-\bigl(t\bm{\Sigma}-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\bigr)\Bigr]P

\displaystyle=2\Bigl[-2\bigl(t\bm{\Sigma}-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\bigr)P+

\displaystyle\quad PP^{\top}\bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)P+\bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)PP^{\top}P\Bigr]

Similarly, let
\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}P=q_{k}^{\top}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\nabla_{P}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=2\Bigl[-2\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\bigl(t\bm{\Sigma}-(1-t)\mathbf{I}\bigr)P+

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}PP^{\top}\bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)P+\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\bigl(t^{2}\bm{\Sigma}+(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{I}\bigr)PP^{\top}P\Bigr]

\displaystyle=2\Bigl[-2\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}P+q_{k}^{\top}\sum_{m}P^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{m}\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{m}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\mathbf{u}_{m}^{\top}P

\displaystyle\quad+\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}P\sum_{n}P^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{n}\mathbf{u}_{n}^{\top}P\Bigr]

\displaystyle=2\Bigl[-2\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)q_{k}^{\top}+q_{k}^{\top}\sum_{m}q_{m}\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{m}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)q_{m}^{\top}+\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)q_{k}^{\top}\sum_{n}q_{n}q_{n}^{\top}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\nabla_{q_{k}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-4\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)q_{k}+2\sum_{m}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{m}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)q_{m}+2\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\sum_{n}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{n})q_{n}

\displaystyle=-4\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)q_{k}+2\sum_{m}\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{m}+(1-t)^{2}+t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\ (q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})q_{m}

\displaystyle=-4\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)q_{k}+2\sum_{m}\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{m}+t^{2}\lambda_{k}+2(1-t)^{2}\bigr)\ (q_{k}^{\top}q_{m})\ q_{m}

(407)

Simplifying assumption: aligned initialization

Assume at initialization
q_{k}^{\top}q_{m}=0,\forall k\neq m
:

\nabla_{q_{k}}\mathcal{L}=-4\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)q_{k}+4\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})q_{k}

(408)

The learning dynamics follow:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}q_{k}

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla_{q_{k}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=4\eta\Big[\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)-\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})\Big]q_{k}

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{d\tau}(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})=4\eta\Big[\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)-\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)(q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})\Big](q_{k}^{\top}q_{k})

(409)

Using abbreviations:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:=8\eta\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle:=8\eta\bigl(t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle r(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\|q_{k}\|^{2}

we can see the core structure:

\frac{dr(\tau)}{d\tau}=Ar-Br^{2}

(410)

With initialization
r(0)=q_{k}^{\top}q_{k}(\tau=0)=Q_{k}
, the solution reads:

\displaystyle\|q_{k}\|^{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{A}{B}\frac{1}{1+(\frac{A}{BQ_{k}}-1)e^{-A\tau}}

\displaystyle=\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\frac{Q_{k}}{Q_{k}+(\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)}}

\displaystyle=Q_{k}^{*}\frac{Q_{k}}{Q_{k}+(Q_{k}^{*}-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)}}

(411)

where

Q_{k}^{*}=\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}

(412)

So the overall weight dynamics read:

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}(\tau;t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\|q_{k}\|^{2}(\tau)\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{Q_{k}}{Q_{k}+(Q_{k}^{*}-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)}}Q_{k}^{*}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{Q_{k}}{Q_{k}+(Q_{k}^{*}-Q_{k})e^{-8\eta\tau\bigl(t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)\bigr)}}\ \frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

(413)

Note that the optimal weight (
412
) is not positive definite, but the two-layer symmetric weight network is.
So, different from the diffusion case with denoiser loss, there are two scenarios:

•

When
t>\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}+1}
,
A>0
and
Q^{*}>0
. The solution converges to
Q^{*}
:
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\|q_{k}\|^{2}(\tau)=Q_{k}^{*}
.

•

When
t<\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}+1}
,
A<0
and
Q^{*}<0
. In this case, the optimal solution is “non-achievable” by a two-layer symmetric network.
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}e^{-A\tau}\to\infty
, so
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\|q_{k}\|^{2}(\tau)=0
.
In other words,
0

becomes a stable fixed point instead of
Q^{*}
, and the solution
\|q_{k}\|^{2}(\tau)
will be attracted to and stuck at
0
.

Thus,

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\mathbf{W}(\tau;t)=\sum_{k,\ \text{where}\ \lambda_{k}<\frac{1}{t}-1}\frac{t\lambda_{k}-(1-t)}{t^{2}\lambda_{k}+(1-t)^{2}}\mathbf{u}_{k}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}

(414)

Because of this, asymptotically speaking, the symmetric network architecture

P^{\top}P
will not approximate this vector field very well.

Thus, for the purpose of studying the learning dynamics of flow matching
models, some extension beyond the symmetric two-layer linear network is required for a thorough analysis.

Appendix I
Detailed Experimental Procedure

I.1
Computational Resources

All experiments were conducted on research cluster. Model training was performed on single A100 / H100 GPU. MLP training experiments took 20mins-2hrs while CNN based UNet training experiments took 5-8 hours, using around 20GB RAM.

Evaluations were also done on single A100 / H100 GPU, with heavy covariance computation done with CUDA and trajectory plotting and fitting on CPU. Covariance computation for generated samples generally took a few minutes.

I.2
MLP architecture inspired by UNet

We used the following custom architecture inspired by UNet in
[

]
and
[
52
]
paper. The basic block is the following

I.3
EDM Loss Function

We employ the loss function
\mathcal{L}_{\text{EDM}}
introduced in the Elucidated Diffusion Model (EDM) paper
[
32
]
, which is one specific weighting scheme for training diffusion models.

For each data point
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the loss is computed as follows. The noise level for each data point is sampled from a log-normal distribution with hyperparameters
P_{\text{mean}}
and
P_{\text{std}}
(e.g.,
P_{\text{mean}}=-1.2
and
P_{\text{std}}=1.2
). Specifically, the noise level
\sigma
is sampled via

\sigma=\exp\big(P_{\text{mean}}+P_{\text{std}}\,\epsilon\big),\quad\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1).

The weighting function per noise scale is defined as:

w(\sigma)=\frac{\sigma^{2}+\sigma_{\text{data}}^{2}}{\left(\sigma\,\sigma_{\text{data}}\right)^{2}},

with hyperparameter
\sigma_{\text{data}}
(e.g.,
\sigma_{\text{data}}=0.5
).
The noisy input
\mathbf{y}
is created by the following,

\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{x}+\sigma\mathbf{n},\quad\mathbf{n}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(\mathbf{0},\,\mathbf{I}_{d}\big),

Let
D_{\theta}(\mathbf{y},\sigma,\text{labels})
denote the output of the denoising network when given the noisy input
\mathbf{y}
, the noise level
\sigma
, and optional conditioning labels. The EDM loss per data point can be computed as:

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x})=w(\sigma)\,\|D_{\theta}(\mathbf{x}+\sigma\mathbf{n},\sigma,\text{labels})-\mathbf{x}\|^{2}.

Taking expectation over the data points and noise scales, the overall loss reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{EDM}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim p_{data}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{d})}\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\Big[w(\sigma)\,\|D_{\theta}(\mathbf{x}+\sigma\mathbf{n},\sigma,\text{labels})-\mathbf{x}\|^{2}\Big]

(415)

⬇

I.4
Experiment 1: Diffusion Learning of High-dimensional Gaussian Data

I.4.1
Data Generation and Covariance Specification

We consider learning a score-based generative model on synthetic data drawn from a high-dimensional Gaussian distribution of dimension
d=128,256,512
.
Specifically, we first sample a vector of variances

\bm{\sigma}^{2}=\bigl(\sigma_{1}^{2},\sigma_{2}^{2},\dots,\sigma_{d}^{2}\bigr),

where each
\sigma_{i}^{2}
is drawn from a log-normal distribution (implemented via
torch.exp(torch.randn(…))
).
We then sort them in descending order and normalize these variances to have mean equals 1 to fix the overall scale. Denoting

\mathbf{D}=\mathrm{diag}\bigl(\sigma_{1}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{d}^{2}\bigr),

we generate a random rotation matrix
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
by performing a QR decomposition of a matrix of i.i.d. Gaussian entries. This allows us to construct the covariance

\bm{\Sigma}\;=\;\mathbf{R}\,\mathbf{D}\,\mathbf{R}^{\mathsf{T}}.

This rotation matrix
\mathbf{R}
is the eigenbasis of the true covariance matrix. To obtain training samples
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we draw
\mathbf{x}_{i}
from
\mathcal{N}\bigl(\mathbf{0},\bm{\Sigma}\bigr)
. In practice, we generate a total of
10{,}000
samples and stack them as
pnts
. We compute the empirical covariance of the training set,

\bm{\Sigma}_{\text{emp}}=\mathrm{Cov}(\texttt{pnts}),
and verify that it is close to the prescribed true covariance
\bm{\Sigma}
.

I.4.2
Network Architecture and Training Setup

We train a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) to approximate the noise conditional score function.
The base network, implemented as

model=UNetBlockStyleMLP_backbone(ndim=d, nlayers=5, nhidden=256, time_embed_dim=256)

maps a data vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a time embedding
\tau
to a vector of the same dimension
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This backbone is then wrapped in an EDM-style preconditioner via:

\texttt{model\_precd}=\texttt{EDMPrecondWrapper}(\texttt{model},\sigma_{\text{data}}=0.5,\,\sigma_{\text{min}}=0.002,\,\sigma_{\text{max}}=80,\,\rho=7.0),

which standardizes and scales the input according to the EDM framework
[
32
]
.

We use EDM loss with hyperparameters
\texttt{P\_mean}=-1.2
,
\texttt{P\_std}=1.2
, and
\sigma_{\text{data}}=0.5
.
We train the model for
5000
steps using mini-batches of size
1024
. The Adam optimizer is used with a learning rate
\texttt{lr}=10^{-4}
. Each training step processes a batch of data from
pnts
, adds noise with randomized noise scales, and backpropagates through the EDM loss. The loss values at each training steps are recorded.

I.4.3
Sampling and Trajectory Visualization

To visualize the sampling evolution, we sample from the diffusion model using the Heun’s 2nd order deterministic sampler, starting from
\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d})

\displaystyle\texttt{edm\_sampler}(\texttt{model},\mathbf{z},\texttt{num\_steps}=20,\,\sigma_{\text{min}}=0.002,\,\sigma_{\text{max}}=80,\,\rho=7).

We store these samples in
sample_store
to track how sampled distribution evolves over training.

I.4.4
Covariance Evaluation in the True Eigenbasis

To measure how well the trained model captures the true covariance structure, we compute the sample covariance from the final generated samples, denoted
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\text{sample}}
. We then project
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\text{sample}}
and the true
\bm{\Sigma}
into the eigenbasis of
\bm{\Sigma}
. Specifically, letting
\mathbf{R}
be the rotation used above, we compute

\mathbf{R}^{\mathsf{T}}\,\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\text{sample}}\,\mathbf{R}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{R}^{\mathsf{T}}\,\bm{\Sigma}\,\mathbf{R}.

Since
\bm{\Sigma}=\mathbf{R}\,\mathbf{D}\,\mathbf{R}^{\mathsf{T}}
is diagonal in that basis, we then compare the diagonal elements of

\mathbf{R}^{\mathsf{T}}\,\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\text{sample}}\,\mathbf{R}

with
\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{D})
. As training proceeds, we track the ratio

\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{R}^{\mathsf{T}}\,\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\text{sample}}\,\mathbf{R})/\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{D})

to observe convergence toward 1 across the spectrum.

All intermediate results, including loss values and sampled trajectories, are stored to disk for later analysis.

I.5
Experiment 2: Diffusion Learning of MNIST | MLP

I.5.1
Data Preprocessing

For our second experiment, we apply the same EDM architecture to several natural image datasets: MNIST, CIFAR, AFHQ32, FFHQ32, FFHQ32-fixword, FFHQ32-randomword. All dataset except for MNIST are RGB images with 32 resolution, while MNIST is BW images with 28 resolution. These images were flattened as vectors, (784d for MNIST, 3072 for others) and stacked as
pnts
matrix.
We normalize these intensities from
[0,1]
to
[-1,1]
by
\mathbf{x}\;\mapsto\;\frac{\mathbf{x}-0.5}{0.5}.

The resulting data tensor
pnts
is then transferred to GPU memory for training, and we estimate its empirical covariance
\bm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{emp}}=\mathrm{Cov}(\texttt{pnts})
for reference.

I.5.2
Network Architecture and Training Setup

Since the natural dataset is higher dimensional than the synthetic data in the previous experiment, we use a deeper MLP network:
For MNIST:

model

\displaystyle=\texttt{UNetBlockStyleMLP\_backbone}(\texttt{ndim}=784,\texttt{nlayers}=8,\texttt{nhidden}=1024,\texttt{time\_embed\_dim}=128).

For others

model

\displaystyle=\texttt{UNetBlockStyleMLP\_backbone}(\texttt{ndim}=3072,\texttt{nlayers}=8,\texttt{nhidden}=3072,\texttt{time\_embed\_dim}=128).

We again wrap this MLP in an EDM preconditioner:

\texttt{model\_precd}=\texttt{EDMPrecondWrapper}(\texttt{model},\,\sigma_{\text{data}}=0.5,\,\sigma_{\text{min}}=0.002,\,\sigma_{\text{max}}=80,\,\rho=7.0).

The model is trained using the
EDMLoss
described in the previous section, with parameters
\texttt{P\_mean}=-1.2
,
\texttt{P\_std}=1.2
, and
\sigma_{\text{data}}=0.5
. We set the training hyperparameters to
\texttt{lr}=10^{-4}
,
\texttt{n\_steps}=100000
, and
\texttt{batch\_size}=2048
.

I.5.3
Sampling and Analysis

As before, we define a callback function
sampling_callback_fn
that periodically draws i.i.d. Gaussian noise

\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{784})

and applies the EDM sampler to produce generated samples. These intermediate samples are stored in

sample_store
for later analysis.

In addition, we assess convergence of the mean of the generated samples by computing

\|\mathbb{E}[\texttt{x\_out}]\;-\;\mathbb{E}[\texttt{pnts}]\|^{2},

and we track how this mean-squared error evolves over training steps. We also examine the sample covariance
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\text{sample}}
of the final outputs, comparing its diagonal in a given eigenbasis to a target spectrum (e.g. the diagonal variances of the training data or a reference covariance).

All trajectories and intermediate statistics are saved to disk for further inspection.
In particular, we plot the difference between
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\text{sample}}
and
\bm{\Sigma}
in an eigenbasis to illustrate whether the learned samples capture the underlying covariance structure of the training data.

I.6
Experiment 3: Diffusion learning of Image Datasets with EDM-style CNN UNet

We used model configuration similar to
https://github.com/NVlabs/edm
, but with simplified training code more similar to previous experiments.

For the MNIST dataset, we trained a UNet-based CNN (with four blocks, each containing one layer, no attention, and channel multipliers of 1, 2, 3, and 4) on MNIST for 50,000 steps using a batch size of 2,048, a learning rate of
10^{-4}
, 16 base model channels, and an evaluation sample size of 5,000.

For the CIFAR-10 dataset, we trained a UNet model (with three blocks, each containing one layer, wide channels of size 128, and attention at resolution 16) for 50,000 steps using a batch size of 512, a learning rate of
10^{-4}
, and an evaluation sample size of 2,000 (evaluated in batches of 1,024) with 20 sampling steps.

For the AFHQ, FFHQ (32 pixels) dataset, we used the same UNet architecture and training setup, with four blocks, wide channels of size 128, and attention at resolution 8, trained for 50,000 steps with a batch size of 256 and a learning rate of
1\times 10^{-4}
. Evaluation was conducted on 2,000 samples in batches of 512.

For the AFHQ, FFHQ (64 pixels) dataset, we trained a UNet model with four blocks (each containing one layer, wide channels of size 128, and attention at resolution 8) for 250,000 steps using a batch size of 256, a learning rate of
1\times 10^{-4}
, and an evaluation sample size of 2,000 (evaluated in batches of 512).

I.7
Architectural Ablation: Diffusion Learning of Image Datasets with EDM-style CNN ResNet

To systematically examine the effects of network depth and width on diffusion learning dynamics, we conducted a controlled set of experiments using EDM-style CNN ResNet architectures trained on the FFHQ-32
\times
32 dataset.

We designed a simplified single-resolution ResNet denoiser (
SongUNetResNet
) without skip connections or attention. It consists of a stack of residual convolutional blocks conditioned on positional timestep embeddings, followed by a normalization and output convolution. This minimal EDM-style architecture isolates the effects of
depth and width
on diffusion learning dynamics.

All models followed the EDM training configuration
[
32
]
with simplified code consistent with previous experiments, and were trained for 50,000 steps using a batch size of 256, Adam optimizer, a learning rate of
1\times 10^{-4}
. 2,000 samples are generated at given training steps for evaluation. No attention layers were used.

We systematically varied two architectural factors:
(1)
network depth
, by adjusting the number of residual layers per block (
L\in\{1,2,3,5\}
); and
(2)
network width
, by varying the base channel dimension (
C\in\{4,6,8,12,16,32,128,256\}
).
Each configuration was trained independently with identical optimizer settings and no data augmentations to isolate the contribution of architecture to convergence and spectral scaling behavior.
